The Local Langlands Correspondence for GL n \GL_{n} over function fields
Siyan Daniel Li-Huerta

Abstract
. Let F F be a local field of characteristic p > 0 p>0 . By adapting methods of Scholze [ 44 ] , we give a new proof of the local Langlands correspondence for GL n \GL_{n} over F F . More specifically, we construct ℓ \ell -adic Galois representations associated with many discrete automorphic representations over global function fields, which we use to construct a map π ↦ rec ⁡ ( π ) \pi\mapsto\rec(\pi) from isomorphism classes of irreducible smooth representations of GL n ⁡ ( F ) \GL_{n}(F) to isomorphism classes of n n -dimensional semisimple continuous representations of W F W_{F} . Our map rec \rec is characterized in terms of a local compatibility condition on traces of a certain test function f τ , h f_{\tau,h} , and we prove that rec \rec equals the usual local Langlands correspondence (after forgetting the monodromy operator).



Introduction

We start by recalling the
local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
over non-archimedean local fields. Let
F
be a nonarchimedean local field, write
\kappa
for its residue field, and fix a prime number
\ell\neq\pchar\kappa
. The local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
over
F
posits a collection of
canonical
bijections

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{tabular}[]{c}isomorphism classes of irreducible\\
smooth representations of $\GL_{n}(F)$ over $\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}$\end{tabular}\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{tabular}[]{c}isomorphism classes of $n$-dimensional continuous\\
Frobenius-semisimple representations of $W_{F}$ over $\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}$\end{tabular}\right\},

where
W_{F}
denotes the Weil group of
F
with respect to a fixed separable closure of
F
, and
n
ranges over all positive integers. By canonical, we mean they are characterized by recovering local class field theory when
n=1
, being compatible with central characters and duals, and preserving
L
-functions and
\epsilon
-factors of pairs.

To prove such a correspondence, work of Bernstein–Zelevinsky allows us to reduce to the case of cuspidal representations on the automorphic side and irreducible representations on the Galois side. More precisely, they show
[
49
, 10.3]
that one can uniquely reconstruct bijections as above from their restrictions to cuspidal representations

(
\diamond
)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{tabular}[]{c}isomorphism classes of irreducible\\
cuspidal representations of $\GL_{n}(F)$ over $\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}$\end{tabular}\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{tabular}[]{c}isomorphism classes of irreducible continuous\\
$n$-dimensional representations of $W_{F}$ over $\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}$\end{tabular}\right\}.

As alluded to above, work of Henniart
[
22
, Theorem 1.2]
indicates that there is at most one collection of bijections as in Equation (

⋄

) satisfying our canonicity requirements. This ensures the
uniqueness
of the local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
over
F
.

As for its
existence
, such bijections were first proved for function fields by Laumon–Rapoport–Stuhler
[
35
, (15.7)]
and for
p
-adic fields by Harris–Taylor
[
20
, Theorem A]
and Henniart
[
25
, 1.2]
. When
F
is a
p
-adic field, Scholze gave a new proof and characterization
[
44
, Theorem 1.2]
of the local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
over
F
, simplifying arguments of Harris–Taylor.

By adapting the methods of Scholze’s proof, the goal of this paper is to give a new proof and characterization of the local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
when
F
is a function field. Thus, let us henceforth assume that
p\coloneqq\pchar{F}>0
. Grothendieck’s
\ell
-adic monodromy theorem
[
45
, Appendix]
implies that irreducible continuous
n
-dimensional representations of
W_{F}
over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
are smooth, so by fixing a field isomorphism
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}={\mathbb{C}}
, Equation (

⋄

) remains unchanged if we replace
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
with
{\mathbb{C}}
. Unless otherwise specified, all subsequent representations shall be taken over
{\mathbb{C}}
.

We begin by motivating this new characterization of the local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
over
F
, as in the work of Scholze. Since
n
-dimensional semisimple continuous representations of
W_{F}
are determined by their traces, it would be natural to characterize the correspondence via a trace condition. More precisely, one would like to construct a map
\pi\mapsto\rho(\pi)
from isomorphism classes of irreducible smooth representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
to isomorphism classes of
n
-dimensional semisimple continuous representations of
W_{F}
satisfying the following condition: for all
\tau
in
W_{F}
, there exists a test function
f_{\tau}
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F))
such that

\displaystyle\tr(f_{\tau}|\pi)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi)\right)

for all irreducible smooth representations
\pi
of
\GL_{n}(F)
.

However, this is too much to ask for, as noted by Scholze in
[
44
]
. To see this, note that we want
\rho(\pi)
to be (a Tate twist of) the Weil representation corresponding to
\pi
under the local Langlands correspondence. But if this were the case, then the above equation implies that
f_{\tau}
has nonzero trace on
every
component of the Bernstein center of
\GL_{n}(F)
. This is impossible because
f_{\tau}
is locally constant.
1
1

1

However, such an
f_{\tau}

does
exist as an element of the Bernstein center and hence as a distribution. See Proposition
2.10
.

We smooth out this issue by convolving
f_{\tau}
with a cut-off function. To elaborate, let us introduce some notation: write
q
for
\#\kappa
, and write
v\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W_{F}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{Z}}
for the unramified homomorphism sending geometric
q
-Frobenii to
1
. For any
\tau
in
W_{F}
with
v(\tau)>0
and
h
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))
, we construct a test function
f_{\tau,h}
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F))
satisfying the following theorem.

Theorem A
.

Let
n
be a positive integer, and let
\pi
be any irreducible smooth representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
.

(i)

There exists a unique
n
-dimensional semisimple continuous representation
\rho(\pi)
of
W_{F}
satisfying the following property:

\displaystyle\mbox{for all }\tau\mbox{ in }W_{F}\mbox{ with }v(\tau)>0\mbox{ and }h\mbox{ in }C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))\mbox{, we have }\tr(f_{\tau,h}|\pi)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi)\right)\tr(h|\pi).

Write
\rec(\pi)
for
\rho(\pi)(\frac{1-n}{2})
, where
(s)
denotes the
s
-th Tate twist.

(ii)

Suppose
\pi
is isomorphic to a subquotient of the normalized parabolic induction of

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{t},

where the
\pi_{i}
are irreducible smooth representations of
\GL_{n_{i}}(F)
such that
n_{1}+\dotsb+n_{t}=n
. Then

\displaystyle\rec(\pi)=\rec(\pi_{1})\oplus\dotsb\oplus\rec(\pi_{t}).

Theorem A.(i) indicates that
f_{\tau,h}
satisfies the trace compatibility property we would expect from the convolution
f_{\tau}*h
. As our notation suggests, we shall see in Theorem C that
\pi\mapsto\rec(\pi)
equals the usual local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
over
F
(after forgetting the monodromy operator). Thus this gives a new characterization of the local Langlands correspondence in this case.

How can we find such an
f_{\tau,h}
? Let
\overline{\kappa}
be a fixed separable closure of
\kappa
. The Deligne–Carayol conjecture (which was proven for function fields by Boyer
[
10
, Theorem 3.2.4]
and for
p
-adic fields by Harris–Taylor
[
20
, Theorem B]
) indicates that, roughly speaking, the local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
as in Equation (

⋄

) can be found in the cohomology of deformation spaces of certain
1
-dimensional formal
{\mathcal{O}}
-modules over
\overline{\kappa}
. By the Dieudonné equivalence,
1
-dimensional formal
{\mathcal{O}}
-modules correspond to special examples of
1
-dimensional effective minuscule local shtukas
. The deformation spaces of general
1
-dimensional effective minuscule local shtukas can be pieced together from those of
1
-dimensional formal
{\mathcal{O}}
-modules (see §
1
), and this mirrors how general irreducible smooth representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
can be pieced together from cuspidal ones via parabolic induction (see §
3
).

Therefore, to find a test function
f_{\tau,h}
satisfying Theorem A, we study deformation spaces of
1
-dimensional effective minuscule local shtukas. We start by parameterizing these objects as follows. Write
r\coloneqq v(\tau)
, write
F_{r}
for the
r
-th degree unramified extension of
F
, write
{\mathcal{O}}_{r}
for its ring of integers, and write
\kappa_{r}
for its residue field. Then the Cartan decomposition for
\GL_{n}(F_{r})
shows that isomorphism classes of
1
-dimensional effective minuscule local shtukas over
\kappa_{r}
correspond to elements

\displaystyle\delta\in\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})

up to
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})
-
\sigma
-conjugacy, where
\varpi
is a uniformizer of
F
. Form the deformation space of the corresponding
1
-dimensional effective minuscule local shtuka with Drinfeld level-
m
structure, and write
R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m}
for the
i
-th
\ell
-adic cohomology group of the adic generic fiber of this deformation space. As
m
varies, we use these cohomology groups to construct representations

\displaystyle R^{i}\psi_{\delta}\coloneqq\varinjlim_{m}R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m}\mbox{ and }[R\psi_{\delta}]\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{i}R^{i}\psi_{\delta},

which have commuting actions of
W_{F_{r}}
and
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
. From here, we can first define a function
\phi_{\tau,h}
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F_{r}))
by sending

\displaystyle\delta\mapsto\begin{cases}\tr(\tau\times h|[R\psi_{\delta}])&\mbox{if }\delta\mbox{ is in }\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r}),\\
0&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{cases}

and then we let
f_{\tau,h}
be a transfer of
\phi_{\tau,h}
to
\GL_{n}(F)
.

Now that we have our test function
f_{\tau,h}
, let’s discuss the proof of Theorem A. After constructing
\rho(\pi)
in special cases, we use the geometry of our deformation spaces, along with work of Bernstein–Zelevinsky
[
49
]
on the local automorphic side, to inductively piece together
\rho(\pi)
in the general case. The process of piecing together
\rho(\pi)
amounts entirely to local deformation theory and nonarchimedean harmonic analysis. This proceeds as in
[
44
]
, except the necessary harmonic analysis is more difficult in characteristic
p
. Therefore we carefully give the argument and supply references for the relevant harmonic analysis in characteristic
p
.

We use global techniques to construct
\rho(\pi)
in special cases. But instead of Shimura varieties as in
[
44
]
, we use moduli spaces of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves
, the latter of which is an equi-characteristic analogue of abelian varieties equipped with certain endomorphisms. For good reduction, these moduli spaces were first considered by Laumon–Rapoport–Stuhler
[
35
]
in their proof of the local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
over
F
, and we shall also crucially use a version of these moduli spaces with bad reduction (which corresponds to using Drinfeld level structures) as considered by Boyer
[
10
]
in his proof of the Deligne–Carayol conjecture for
F
.

We explicitly define
\rho(\pi)
as a multiplicity space in the cohomology of the moduli space of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves. To show that
\rho(\pi)
satisfies our desired trace compatibility condition, we adapt Scholze’s version of the Langlands–Kottwitz method to compute traces of Frobenius and Hecke operators on the aforementioned cohomology groups. These traces are related to our test function
f_{\tau,h}
via a Serre–Tate theorem for
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves, which equates deformations of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves to deformations of certain associated local shtukas. For this, it is crucial to carry out the Langlands–Kottwitz method at bad reduction, which is new in positive characteristic.

This global work yields the following construction of
\ell
-adic Galois representations associated with certain discrete automorphic representations. Let
\mathbf{F}
be a global function field, write
{\mathbb{A}}
for its ring of adeles, and let
\{x_{1},x_{2},\infty\}
be three distinct places of
\mathbf{F}
. Write
G_{\mathbf{F}}
for the absolute Galois group of
\mathbf{F}
with respect to a fixed separable closure of
\mathbf{F}
.

Theorem B
.

Let
\Pi
be an irreducible discrete automorphic representation of
\GL_{n}({\mathbb{A}})
whose components at
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
, and
\infty
are either irreducible
L^{2}
representations or Speh modules. Then there exists a unique
n
-dimensional semisimple continuous representation
R(\Pi)
of
G_{\mathbf{F}}
over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
such that, for all places
o
of
\mathbf{F}
not lying in
\{x_{1},x_{2},\infty\}
, the restriction of
R(\Pi)
to
W_{\mathbf{F}_{o}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left.R(\Pi)\right|_{W_{\mathbf{F}_{o}}}=\rho(\Pi_{o}),

where we identify
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
with
{\mathbb{C}}
.

We finish the construction of
\rho(\pi)
by finding a
\Pi
as above such that
\pi
is isomorphic to
\Pi_{o}
, and then applying Theorem B. In general, this is more difficult in characteristic
p
because the trace formula is not as developed, but we circumvent this using Mœglin–Waldspurger’s description of the discrete automorphic spectrum. This concludes our construction of
\rho(\pi)
in special cases and thus our proof of Theorem A.

From here, we prove the following bijectivity result.

Theorem C
.

The map
\pi\mapsto\rec(\pi)
yields a bijection from isomorphism classes of irreducible cuspidal representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
to isomorphism classes of
n
-dimensional irreducible continuous representations of
W_{F}
.

The key ingredient is an explicit calculation of the inertia invariants of nearby cycles due to Scholze
[
42
, Theorem 5.3]
, which relies on a case of Grothendieck’s purity conjecture as proved by Thomason
[
47
, Corollary 3.9]
. We use this calculation to deduce that if
\rec(\pi)
is unramified, then
\pi
must be as well. By passing to the Galois side and using our work on local-global compatibility, we show that after applying cyclic base change
[
27
, (II.1.4)]
finitely many times, the representation
\pi
becomes unramified. Passing to this unramified representation ultimately allows us to prove Theorem C. As in
[
44
]
, this argument bypasses the need to appeal to Henniart’s numerical local Langlands
[
24
, Theorem 1.2]
.

Finally, we show that
\pi\mapsto\rec(\pi)
satisfies the usual canonicity requirements of the local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
.

Theorem D
.

The bijections

\displaystyle\rec\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\left\{\begin{tabular}[]{c}isomorphism classes of irreducible\\
cuspidal representations of $\GL_{n}(F)$\end{tabular}\right\}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\left\{\begin{tabular}[]{c}isomorphism classes of $n$-dimensional\\
irreducible continuous representations of $W_{F}$\end{tabular}\right\}

satisfy the following properties:

(i)

for all irreducible cuspidal representations of
\GL_{1}(F)
, that is, smooth characters
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F^{\times}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
, we have

\displaystyle\rec(\chi)=\chi\circ\Art^{-1},

where
\Art
denotes the Artin isomorphism
\Art\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F^{\times}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}W^{\text{ab}}_{F}
that sends uniformizers to geometric
q
-Frobenii.

(ii)

for all irreducible cuspidal representations
\pi
of
\GL_{n}(F)
and smooth characters
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F^{\times}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
, we have

\displaystyle\rec(\pi\otimes(\chi\circ\operatorname{det}))=\rec(\pi)\otimes\rec(\chi).

(iii)

for all irreducible cuspidal representations
\pi
of
\GL_{n}(F)
with central character
\omega_{\pi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F^{\times}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
, we have

\displaystyle\rec(\omega_{\pi})=\operatorname{det}\circ\rec(\pi)\mbox{ and }\rec(\pi^{\vee})=\rec(\pi)^{\vee}.

(iv)

for all irreducible cuspidal representations
\pi
of
\GL_{n}(F)
and
\pi^{\prime}
of
\GL_{n^{\prime}}(F)
, we have

\displaystyle L(\pi\times\pi^{\prime},s)=L(\rec(\pi)\otimes\rec(\pi^{\prime}),s)\mbox{ and }\epsilon(\pi\times\pi^{\prime},\psi,s)=\epsilon(\rec(\pi)\otimes\rec(\pi^{\prime}),\psi,s)

for all nontrivial continuous homomorphisms
\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
.

By
[
22
, Theorem 1.2]
, Theorem D shows that our construction indeed equals the usual local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
.

We now discuss the proof of Theorem D. Compatibility with local class field theory follows from earlier work, as our use of
1
-dimensional formal
{\mathcal{O}}
-modules recovers the Lubin–Tate description of local class field theory when
n=1
. Compatibility with twists of characters follows by embedding into the global setting, using Theorem B, and applying the strong multiplicity one theorem
[
6
, Theorem 3.3.(b)]
. We prove compatibility with central characters via similar global means—this time, we reduce to the situation of induced representations by Brauer induction, and then we prove some cases of non-Galois automorphic induction in order to pass to the automorphic side. From here, Henniart’s trick of twisting by highly ramified characters
[
23
, Lemma 4.2]
implies compatibility with
L
-functions and
\epsilon
-factors, and then compatibility with duals follows from the decomposition of
L
-functions of pairs in terms of
L
-functions of characters.

Outline

In §
1
, we introduce local shtukas, the geometry of their deformation spaces, and the test function
f_{\tau,h}
. Next, in §
2
, we recall the Bernstein–Zelevinsky classification, the Bernstein center, and Schneider–Zink tempered types. We use this to prove Lemma
2.7
, which serves as the framework for proving Theorem A. The primary goal of §
3
–§
8
is to prove the ingredients needed for running Lemma
2.7
. In §
3
, we use the geometry of deformation spaces along with the Bernstein–Zelevinsky induction-restriction formula to prove compatibility with parabolic induction. In §
4
, we compare our deformation spaces with the Lubin–Tate tower. With the exception of Lemma
1.14
, the entirety of §
1
–§
4
is local.

At this point, we switch to a global setup. In §
5
, we introduce
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves, their moduli spaces, and their relationship with local shtukas. In §
6
, we present an explicit calculation of nearby cycle sheaves on the special fibers of these moduli spaces at bad reduction. In §
7
, we employ a variant of the Langlands–Kottwitz method, using the Serre–Tate theorem as introduced in
5.21
to relate the cohomology of the moduli space of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves with the local test function
f_{\tau,h}
. In §
8
, we deduce Theorem B from §
7
, and then we embed our local situation into our global one to finish the proof of Theorem A.

We now turn to the proofs of Theorem C and Theorem D. In §
9
, we recall cyclic base change, and we combine this with local-global embedding theorems to prove Theorem C. In §
10
, we use Theorem B (as well as compatibility with automorphic induction) to prove some cases of non-Galois automorphic induction. We tie these results together to prove Theorem D, concluding our proof of the local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
over
F
.

Notation

Let
\ell\neq p
be a prime number. We fix an identification
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}={\mathbb{C}}
of fields. Unless otherwise specified, all representations are over
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Throughout §
1
–§
4
, let
F
be a local field of positive characteristic. Write
{\mathcal{O}}
for its ring of integers, fix a uniformizer
\varpi
of
{\mathcal{O}}
, and write
\kappa
for the residue field
{\mathcal{O}}/\varpi
. This choice of
\varpi
yields an identification
{\mathcal{O}}=\kappa[\![\varpi]\!]
. We denote
\#\kappa
using
q
. We write
v
for the normalized discrete valuation on
F
, and we denote the normalized valuation on
F
using
{\lvert-\rvert}
. We fix a separable closure
F^{\text{sep}}
in which our separable extensions of
F
lie, and we write
\overline{\kappa}
for the residue field of
F^{\text{sep}}
, which is a separable closure of
\kappa
. We write
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}
for the completion of
F^{\text{sep}}
, and we view completions of separable extensions of
F
as closed subfields of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}
.

We write
W_{F}
for the Weil group of
F
with respect to this choice of
F^{\text{sep}}
, and we view Weil groups of separable extensions of
F
as living inside
W_{F}
. We denote the inertia subgroup of
F
using
I_{F}
. We have a canonical short exact sequence

\displaystyle 1\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}I_{F}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}W_{F}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{v}{\mathbb{Z}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}1,

and we identify
1
in
{\mathbb{Z}}
with the geometric
q
-Frobenius in
\Gal(\overline{\kappa}/\kappa)
. We write
\Art\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F^{\times}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}W_{F}^{\text{ab}}
for the local Artin isomorphism normalized by sending uniformizers to geometric
q
-Frobenii.

1.
Deformation spaces of local shtukas

In this section, we introduce
local shtukas
, which are the equi-characteristic analogue of isocrystals. We then narrow our scope to
effective minuscule
local shtukas, which are the analogue of Dieudonné modules. A similar Dieudonné equivalence relates
1
-dimensional connected local shtukas to
1
-dimensional formal
{\mathcal{O}}
-modules, which allows us to use results of Drinfeld to study their deformation spaces. From here, we deduce finitude properties about the cohomology of these deformation spaces from a global algebraization result, and we conclude by defining the test functions
\phi_{\tau,h}
and
f_{\tau,h}
using these cohomology groups.

1.1.

Given a scheme
S
over
\Spec{\mathcal{O}}
, we write
\zeta
for the image of
\varpi
in
{\mathscr{O}}_{S}
. Note that requiring
\zeta
to be locally nilpotent is the same as requiring
S\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\Spec{\mathcal{O}}
to factor as
S\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\Spf{\mathcal{O}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\Spec{\mathcal{O}}
. We consider
{\mathscr{O}}_{S}[\![\varpi]\!]
and
{\mathscr{O}}_{S}(\!(\varpi)\!)
as sheaves of formal power and Laurent series on
{\lvert S\rvert}
, respectively
[
21
, p. 4]
.

Definition
.

Let
S
be a scheme over
\Spf{\mathcal{O}}
. An
local shtuka
over
S
is a pair
({\mathscr{M}},{\mathscr{F}})
, where

\bullet

{\mathscr{M}}
is a locally free
{\mathscr{O}}_{S}[\![\varpi]\!]
-module of finite rank,

\bullet

{\mathscr{F}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sigma^{*}{\mathscr{M}}[\frac{1}{\varpi}]\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathscr{M}}[\textstyle\frac{1}{\varpi}]
is an
{\mathscr{O}}_{S}(\!(\varpi)\!)
-module isomorphism,

where
\sigma
denotes the absolute
q
-Frobenius on
S
and its canonical lifts to
{\mathscr{O}}_{S}[\![\varpi]\!]
and
{\mathscr{O}}_{S}(\!(\varpi)\!)
. A
morphism
of local shtukas is a morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathscr{M}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathscr{M}}^{\prime}
of locally free
{\mathscr{O}}_{S}[\![\varpi]\!]
-modules satisfying
f[\frac{1}{\varpi}]\circ{\mathscr{F}}={\mathscr{F}}^{\prime}\circ f[\frac{1}{\varpi}]
. A
quasi-isogeny
of local shtukas is an invertible element in
\Hom({\mathscr{M}},{\mathscr{M}}^{\prime})[\frac{1}{\varpi}]
. When such an element exists, we say
{\mathscr{M}}
and
{\mathscr{M}}^{\prime}
are
isogenous
.

1.2.

For any field
\lambda
over
\kappa
, we see that isomorphism classes of local shtukas over
\Spec\lambda
of rank
n
correspond to
\GL_{n}(\lambda[\![\varpi]\!])
-
\sigma
-conjugacy classes in
\GL_{n}(\lambda(\!(\varpi)\!))
via sending
\delta
in
\GL_{n}(\lambda(\!(\varpi)\!))
to
(\lambda[\![\varpi]\!]^{n},\delta\circ\sigma^{\oplus n})
, where
\delta
acts via left multiplication on column vectors. In further analogy with isocrystals, we also have a
Dieudonné–Manin classification

[
34
, (2.4.5)]
for isogeny classes of local shtukas over algebraically closed fields.

1.3 Definition
.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be a local shtuka over
S
. We say that
{\mathscr{M}}
is
effective
if
{\mathscr{F}}
is the localization of an
{\mathscr{O}}_{S}[\![\varpi]\!]
-module morphism
\sigma^{*}{\mathscr{M}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathscr{M}}
, which we henceforth refer to as
{\mathscr{F}}
by abuse of notation. For an effective local shtuka
{\mathscr{M}}
, one can show that
\coker{\mathscr{F}}
is a locally free
{\mathscr{O}}_{S}
-module of finite rank
[
21
, Lemma 2.3]
, and we say the
dimension
of
{\mathscr{M}}
is the rank of
\coker{\mathscr{F}}
. We say
{\mathscr{M}}
is
étale
if its dimension equals zero.

We say
{\mathscr{M}}
is
effective minuscule
if
\varpi-\zeta
annihilates
\coker{\mathscr{F}}
. For an effective minuscule local shtuka
{\mathscr{M}}
, we say
{\mathscr{M}}
is
connected
if
{\mathscr{F}}
is
\varpi
-adically nilpotent.

1.4.

In our above description
1.2
of isomorphism classes of local shtukas over
\Spec\lambda
, we see that the local shtuka corresponding to
\delta
is effective if and only if
\delta
lies in
\M_{n}(\lambda[\![\varpi]\!])
. By the Cartan decomposition, it is effective minuscule if and only if
\delta
lies in

\displaystyle\coprod_{d=0}^{n}\GL_{n}(\lambda[\![\varpi]\!])\diag(\underbrace{\varpi,\dotsc,\varpi}_{d\,\text{times}},1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}(\lambda[\![\varpi]\!]),

where
d
corresponds to the dimension of
(\lambda[\![\varpi]\!]^{n},\delta\circ\sigma^{\oplus n})
.

1.5.

Just as with Dieudonné modules, effective minuscule local shtukas have a good theory of

\bullet

truncated variants, called
finite
\kappa
-shtukas

[
21
, Definition 2.6]
, which we remark can be defined over any scheme
S
over
\Spec{\mathcal{O}}
,

\bullet

a contravariant
Dieudonné equivalence

[
21
, Theorem 5.2]
between finite
\kappa
-shtukas and certain finite module schemes called
strict
\kappa
-modules

[
21
, Definition 4.8]
that extends to an anti-equivalence
[
21
, Theorem 8.3]
between effective minuscule local shtukas and certain module sheaves called
\varpi
-divisible local Anderson modules

[
21
, Definition 7.1]
,

\bullet

a
connected-étale
short exact sequence over local Artinian rings that splits over perfect fields
[
21
, Proposition 2.9]
.

1.6.

Let
\breve{F}
be the completion of the maximal unramified extension of
F
, and write
\breve{\mathcal{O}}
for its ring of integers. Our choice of
\varpi
yields an identification
\breve{\mathcal{O}}=\overline{\kappa}[\![\varpi]\!]
.

We begin by examining the case
S=\Spec\overline{\kappa}
. Let
\delta
be an element of
\GL_{n}(\breve{\mathcal{O}})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}(\breve{\mathcal{O}})
, and write
\breve{H}_{\delta}
for the associated effective minuscule local shtuka over
\Spec\overline{\kappa}
of rank
n
and dimension
1
. The connected-étale sequence yields a decomposition

\displaystyle\breve{H}_{\delta}=\breve{H}_{\delta}^{\circ}\oplus\breve{H}_{\delta}^{\text{\'{e}t}},

where
\breve{H}_{\delta}^{\circ}
and
\breve{H}_{\delta}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
are the connected and étale parts of
\breve{H}_{\delta}
, respectively. Write
k
for the rank of
\breve{H}_{\delta}^{\circ}
.

As
\breve{H}_{\delta}^{\circ}
is the connected part, it must have dimension
1
, so under the Dieudonné equivalence it corresponds to the unique formal
{\mathcal{O}}
-module of height
k
and dimension
1
, i.e. the Lubin–Tate module
[
16
, Proposition 1.7.1)]
. By applying the Dieudonné equivalence to
\breve{H}_{\delta}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
and using the algebraic closedness of
\Spec\overline{\kappa}
, we also see that
\breve{H}_{\delta}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
is the unique étale effective minuscule local shtuka over
\Spec\overline{\kappa}
of rank
n-k
, i.e. the constant
\varpi
-divisible local Anderson module
\underline{(F/{\mathcal{O}})}^{n-k}
. Thus under
1.2
, we see that
\breve{H}_{\delta}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
corresponds to the
\GL_{n-k}(\breve{\mathcal{O}})
-
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
1
, and
H_{\delta}^{\circ}
corresponds to a basic element
\delta^{\circ}
in
\GL_{k}(\breve{\mathcal{O}})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{k}(\breve{\mathcal{O}})
, up to
\GL_{k}(\breve{\mathcal{O}})
-
\sigma
-conjugation.

1.7.

Let
F_{r}
be the
r
-th degree unramified extension of
F
, write
{\mathcal{O}}_{r}
for its ring of integers, and write
\kappa_{r}
for
{\mathcal{O}}_{r}/\varpi
, which is the
r
-degree extension of
\kappa
. Our choice of
\varpi
yields an identification
{\mathcal{O}}_{r}=\kappa_{r}[\![\varpi]\!]
.

We now turn to the case of
S=\Spec\kappa_{r}
. Let
\delta
be an element of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})
, and write
H_{\delta}
for the associated effective minuscule local shtuka over
\Spec\kappa_{r}
of rank
n
and dimension
1
. The connected-étale sequence yields a decomposition

\displaystyle H_{\delta}=H_{\delta}^{\circ}\oplus H_{\delta}^{\text{\'{e}t}},

where
H_{\delta}^{\circ}
and
H^{\text{\'{e}t}}_{\delta}
are the connected and étale parts of
H_{\delta}
, respectively. Write
k
for the rank of
H_{\delta}^{\circ}
. Under
1.2
, we see that
H_{\delta}^{\text{\'{e}t}}
corresponds to some
\delta^{\text{\'{e}t}}
in
\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})
, up to
\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})
-
\sigma
-conjugation, and one can show that
H_{\delta}^{\circ}
corresponds to some
\delta^{\circ}
in
\GL_{k}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{k}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})
, up to
\GL_{k}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})
-
\sigma
-conjugation, whose norm

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}(\delta^{\circ})\coloneqq\delta^{\circ}\sigma(\delta^{\circ})\dotsm\sigma^{r-1}(\delta^{\circ})

is
\GL_{k}(F_{r})
-conjugate to an elliptic element in
\GL_{k}(F)
. We denote the set of such
\delta
by
B_{n,k}
, and we write
B_{k}
for
B_{k,k}
. Note that the pullback of
H_{\delta}
to
\Spec\overline{\kappa}
is isomorphic to
\breve{H}_{\delta}
.

1.8.

To define our deformation spaces of local shtukas (with additional data), we need to introduce a notion of
Drinfeld level structures
for effective local shtukas. For any effective local shtuka
{\mathscr{M}}
, its
m
-th level truncation is
{\mathscr{M}}/\varpi^{m}
. For any finite
\kappa
-shtuka
M
, we write
\Dr(M)
for the corresponding finite
\kappa
-strict module
[
21
, p. 16]
, which is an
{\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m}
-module scheme over
S
.

Definition
.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be an effective minuscule local shtuka over
S
of constant rank
n
. We say a
Drinfeld level-
m
structure
on
{\mathscr{M}}
is a Drinfeld level-
m
structure on
\Dr({\mathscr{M}}/\varpi^{m})
, that is, an
{\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m}
-module morphism

\displaystyle\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\varpi^{-m}{\mathcal{O}}/{\mathcal{O}})^{n}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\Dr({\mathscr{M}}/\varpi^{m})(S)

such that the collection of all
\alpha(x)
for
x
in
(\varpi^{-m}{\mathcal{O}}/{\mathcal{O}})^{n}
forms a full set of sections of
\Dr({\mathscr{M}}/\varpi^{m})
as in
[
30
, (1.8.2)]
. For any Drinfeld level-
m
structure
\alpha
, its restriction to
(\varpi^{-m^{\prime}}{\mathcal{O}}/{\mathcal{O}})^{n}
is a Drinfeld level-
m^{\prime}
structure, which we denote by
\left.\alpha\right|_{m^{\prime}}
. Furthermore, if
S
is the spectrum of a local Artinian ring, then the restriction of
\alpha
to
\ker\alpha
is a Drinfeld level-
m
structure on the connected part
{\mathscr{M}}^{\circ}
of
{\mathscr{M}}
. We denote this by
\alpha^{\circ}
.

1.9.

We now initiate our study of deformation spaces. Write
\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}
for the opposite category of the category whose

\bullet

objects are complete Noetherian local
\breve{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras
A
with residue field
\overline{\kappa}
,

\bullet

morphisms are local
\breve{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra morphisms,

and write
{\mathcal{C}}
for the full subcategory of
\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}
consisting of Artinian rings. We identify
\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}
with a full subcategory of formal schemes over
\Spf\breve{\mathcal{O}}
. Note that for
A
in
{\mathcal{C}}
, we have
\Spf{A}=\Spec{A}
.

For any contravariant functor
E\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathcal{C}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}(\text{Set})
, we write
\widehat{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}(\text{Set})
for the extension of
E
to
\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}
given by sending

\displaystyle A\mapsto\varprojlim_{i}E(A/{\mathfrak{m}}^{i}_{A}),

where
{\mathfrak{m}}_{A}
is the maximal ideal of
A
. We say
E

has a deformation space
if
\widehat{E}
is representable by a finite disjoint union
{\mathfrak{X}}
of formal schemes in
\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}
. By Yoneda’s lemma, such an
{\mathfrak{X}}
is unique up to isomorphism.

1.10.

Return to the situation in
1.6
, and let
\alpha
be a Drinfeld level-
m
structure on
\breve{H}_{\delta}
. Write
\breve{E}_{\delta,\alpha}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathcal{C}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}(\text{Set})
for the functor sending

\displaystyle A\mapsto\{\mbox{triples }(H^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})\}/\sim,

where
H^{\prime}
is a local shtuka over
\Spec{A}
,
\alpha^{\prime}
is a Drinfeld level-
m
structure on
H^{\prime}
, and
\iota^{\prime}
is an isomorphism
\breve{H}_{\delta}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}H^{\prime}_{\overline{\kappa}}
such that
(\alpha^{\prime})_{\overline{\kappa}}=\iota^{\prime}\circ\alpha
. In other words,
\breve{E}_{\delta,\alpha}
parametrizes deformations of
(\breve{H}_{\delta},\alpha)
. Note that
\breve{E}_{\delta,\alpha}
has a right action of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})
given by sending
(H^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
to
(H^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\circ\gamma,\iota^{\prime})
for any
\gamma
in
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})
.

By applying the Dieudonné equivalence to rephrase the problem in terms of
\varpi
-divisible local Anderson modules, we immediately deduce the following from classical results of Drinfeld.

Proposition

(
[
16
, Proposition 4.2]
,
[
16
, Proposition 4.3]
,
[
46
, 2.1.2.(ii)]
)
.

(i)

The functor
\breve{E}_{\delta,\alpha}
has a deformation space, which we denote using
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,\alpha}=\Spf\breve{R}_{\delta,\alpha}
. This
\breve{R}_{\delta,\alpha}
is a regular complete local Noetherian
\breve{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra.

(ii)

Write
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta}=\Spf\breve{R}_{\delta}
for the deformation space with Drinfeld level-
0
structure, that is, no Drinfeld level structure. Choosing a coordinate on
\breve{H}_{\delta}^{\circ}
and an isomorphism
\breve{H}_{\delta}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\breve{H}_{\delta}^{\circ}\oplus\underline{(F/{\mathcal{O}})}^{n-k}
induces an isomorphism from
\breve{R}_{\delta}
to the formal power series ring
\breve{\mathcal{O}}[\![s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n-1}]\!]
.

(iii)

The same choice as in (ii) induces an isomorphism from
\breve{R}_{\delta,\alpha}
to the formal power series ring
\breve{R}_{\delta^{\circ},\alpha^{\circ}}[\![t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n-k}]\!]
, where the
\breve{R}_{\delta^{\circ},\alpha^{\circ}}
-algebra structure is given by the restriction morphism
\breve{R}_{\delta^{\circ},\alpha^{\circ}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{R}_{\delta,\alpha}
.

(iv)

For all non-negative integers
m^{\prime}\leq m
, the restriction morphism
\breve{R}_{\delta,\left.\alpha\right|_{m^{\prime}}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{R}_{\delta,\alpha}
is finite flat. Its generic fiber
\breve{R}_{\delta,\left.\alpha\right|_{m^{\prime}}}[\frac{1}{\varpi}]\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{R}_{\delta,\alpha}[\textstyle\frac{1}{\varpi}]
is a Galois extension of rings, and the left action of

\displaystyle\ker\left(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m^{\prime}})\right)

on
\breve{R}_{\delta,\left.\alpha\right|_{m^{\prime}}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{R}_{\delta,\alpha}
yields the Galois action on
\breve{R}_{\delta,\left.\alpha\right|_{m^{\prime}}}[\frac{1}{\varpi}]\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{R}_{\delta,\alpha}[\textstyle\frac{1}{\varpi}]
.

In particular, parts (ii) and (iv) imply that
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,\alpha}
has dimension
n-1
over
\Spf\breve{\mathcal{O}}
.

1.11.

Let
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{k}
be an
{\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m}
-basis of
\ker\alpha
. After choosing a coordinate on
\breve{H}_{\delta^{\circ}}
, we may identify its
A
-points with
{\mathfrak{m}}_{A}
. Classical results of Drinfeld yield certain local parameters of
\breve{R}_{\delta,\alpha}
which satisfy the following relationship with Drinfeld level-
m
structures.

Proposition

(
[
16
, Proposition 4.3.2)]
)
.

There exist local parameters
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k}
of the regular local ring
\breve{R}_{\delta^{\circ},\alpha^{\circ}}
such that, for any morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\breve{R}_{\delta^{\circ},\alpha^{\circ}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}A
in
\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}
, the image of
x_{i}
under
f
equals the element of
{\mathfrak{m}}_{A}
corresponding to
\alpha^{\prime}(e_{i})
, where
(H^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
is the deformation of
(\breve{H}^{\circ}_{\delta},\alpha^{\circ})
corresponding to
f
.

1.12.

Next, we allow the Drinfeld level-
m
structure to vary. Write
\breve{E}_{\delta,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathcal{C}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}(\text{Set})
for the functor sending

\displaystyle A\mapsto\{\mbox{triples }(H^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})\}/\sim,

where
H^{\prime}
is a local shtuka over
\Spec{A}
,
\alpha^{\prime}
is a Drinfeld level-
m
structure on
H^{\prime}
, and
\iota^{\prime}
is an isomorphism
\breve{H}_{\delta}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}H^{\prime}_{\overline{\kappa}}
. In other words,
\breve{E}_{\delta,m}
parametrizes deformations of
\breve{H}_{\delta}
along with a Drinfeld level-
m
structure. As in
1.10
, our functor
\breve{E}_{\delta,m}
has a right action of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})
.

Because every
\alpha^{\prime}
yields a Drinfeld level-
m
structure
\alpha^{\prime}_{\overline{\kappa}}
of
\breve{H}_{\delta}
, we have

\displaystyle\breve{E}_{\delta,m}=\coprod_{\alpha}\breve{E}_{\delta,\alpha},

where
\alpha
ranges over all Drinfeld level-
m
structures on
\breve{H}_{\delta}
. This disjoint union respects both restriction to the connected component as well as restriction to level-
m^{\prime}
, where
m^{\prime}\leq m
. By decomposing Drinfeld level-
m
structures in terms of the connected-étale sequence, we deduce the following result from Proposition
1.10
.

Proposition
.

The functor
\breve{E}_{\delta,m}
has a deformation space, which we denote using
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
. We have an identification

\displaystyle\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}=\coprod_{\alpha}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,\alpha}=\coprod_{V}\coprod_{\ker\alpha=V}\Spf\breve{R}_{\delta^{\circ},\alpha^{\circ}}[\![t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n-k}]\!],

where
V
ranges over all
{\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m}
-linear direct summands of
(\varpi^{-m}{\mathcal{O}}/{\mathcal{O}})^{n}
with rank
k
. Under the first identification, the restriction morphisms
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m^{\prime}}
equal the disjoint union of the restriction morphisms
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,\alpha}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,\left.\alpha\right|_{m^{\prime}}}
.

Fix such a
V
. Since Drinfeld level-
m
structures on étale group schemes are precisely group isomorphisms
[
30
, (1.8.3)]
, the set of all
\alpha
satisfying
\ker\alpha=V
is a right principal homogeneous space for
\GL((\varpi^{-m}{\mathcal{O}}/{\mathcal{O}})^{n}/V)
.

1.13.

Now suppose that
\delta
lies in
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})
. This will allow us to descend
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
to a formal scheme
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
over
\Spf{\mathcal{O}}_{r}
as follows. Let
\sigma
be any element of
\Gal(\overline{\kappa}/\kappa_{r})
, which we identify with its canonical lift to
\Aut(\breve{\mathcal{O}}/{\mathcal{O}}_{r})
, and write
\sigma
for
\Spec\sigma
by abuse of notation. The automorphism of
\breve{H}_{\delta}=H_{\delta,\overline{\kappa}}
given by
\id_{H_{\delta}}\times_{\kappa_{r}}\sigma
lies over
\sigma
and hence induces an isomorphism
\sigma^{*}\breve{H}_{\delta}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\breve{H}_{\delta}
over
\Spec\overline{\kappa}
, which we denote using
f_{\sigma}
.

Note that
\sigma^{*}\breve{H}_{\delta}
is isomorphic to
\breve{H}_{\sigma(\delta)}
and that
\sigma^{*}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
is isomorphic to
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\sigma(\delta),m}
. We obtain an isomorphism
\varphi_{\sigma}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\sigma^{*}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
by sending
(H^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
to
(H^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}\circ f_{\sigma})
. Since the morphisms
f_{\sigma}
satisfy the cocycle condition, the morphisms
\varphi_{\sigma}
do as well (albeit contravariantly), and thus the
\varphi_{\sigma}
provide Weil descent datum for
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
as in
[
39
, Definition (3.5)]
2
2

2

The definition here is stated for mixed characteristic, but it adapts to equal characteristic by using formal power series instead of Witt vectors.
One can show this Weil descent datum is effective, and we write
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
for the resulting formal scheme over
\Spf{\mathcal{O}}_{r}
. The right action of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})
on
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
commutes with
\varphi_{\sigma}
and hence also descends to a right action on
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
. The restriction morphisms similarly descend to morphisms
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m^{\prime}}
.

1.14.

Let
{\mathfrak{X}}
be a special formal scheme over
\Spf{A}
as in
[
7
, p. 370]
for a complete valuation ring
A
. For any complete nonarchimedean field
K
containing
A
, we write
{\mathfrak{X}}_{K}
for the adic pullback of
{\mathfrak{X}}
to
K

[
7
, p. 370]
. Writing
\lambda
for the residue field of
K
, we denote the adic pullback
3
3

3

These are referred to in
[
7
]
as the generic and special fibers, respectively. Our terminology stems from interpreting them as pullbacks in the category of adic spaces.
of
{\mathfrak{X}}
to
\lambda
by
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\lambda}

[
7
, p. 370]
. These constructions correspond to generic and special fibers, respectively, in the setting of nonarchimedean analytic geometry.

We shall now introduce the cohomology of our deformation spaces. Write
R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m}
for the
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
-vector space
H^{i}({\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m,{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})
. The zero-dimensionality of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m,\overline{\kappa}}
and the nearby cycles spectral sequence
[
7
, Corollary 2.5]
show that

\displaystyle R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m}=H^{0}({\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m,\overline{\kappa}},R^{i}\Psi_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m,{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}}}\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}),

where
R^{i}\Psi_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m,{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}}}
denotes the
i
-th nearby cycles functor
4
4

4

This is referred to in
[
7
]
and
[
20
]
as the vanishing cycles functor.

[
7
, p. 373]
on
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m,{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}}}
. This equality explains our choice of notation for
R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m}
. To prove the finite-dimensionality of
R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m}
as well as eventually prove the admissibility of a certain
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
-action, we take recourse to the following algebraization result.

Lemma
.

There exists a projective scheme
{\mathcal{M}}
over
\Spec\breve{\mathcal{O}}
, a zero-dimensional closed subscheme
{\mathcal{Z}}
of
{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\kappa}}
, and a right action of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})
on the pair
({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{Z}})
such that the completion of
{\mathcal{M}}
at
{\mathcal{Z}}
is isomorphic to
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
with its right action of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})
.

The desired algebraization comes from a moduli space of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves with bad reduction
, but we won’t introduce these moduli spaces until §
5
, and we won’t explain how to relate them to local shtukas until §
7
. Although we record the proof here, one can safely take this algebraization result as a black box.

Proof of Lemma
Lemma
.

Let
C={\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\kappa}
be our curve of interest, and let
\infty
and
o
be distinct
\kappa
-points of
C
. Let
D
be any central division algebra over
\kappa(C)
of dimension
n^{2}
that splits at
o
and
\infty
, and let
{\mathscr{D}}
be a maximal order of
D
, which can be constructed using
5.4
because division algebras split at cofinitely many places. Proposition
7.9
allows us to find a
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaf
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
over
\overline{\kappa}
whose local shtuka
{\mathscr{M}}_{o}^{\prime}
at
o
as in
5.16
is isomorphic to
\breve{H}_{\delta}
.

Denote the corresponding
\overline{\kappa}
-point of
{\mathcal{M}}_{\varnothing,\breve{\mathcal{O}}}
using
z
. Write
o^{m}
for the finite closed subscheme of
C
supported on
o
with multiplicity
m
, and write
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathcal{M}}_{o^{m},\breve{\mathcal{O}}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{M}}_{\varnothing,\breve{\mathcal{O}}}
for the restriction morphism. Then
5.21
says that
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{M}}_{o^{m},\breve{\mathcal{O}}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}=\pi^{-1}(z)
yield the desired algebraization of
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
.
∎

1.15.

With Lemma
1.14
in hand, Berkovich’s nearby cycles comparison theorem
[
7
, Theorem 3.1]
implies that
R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m}
is finite-dimensional over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
and vanishes for
i>n-1
by Proposition
1.10
. Furthermore, it has commuting continuous left actions of
W_{F_{r}}
and
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})
. Write
R^{i}\psi_{\delta}
for the direct limit

\displaystyle R^{i}\psi_{\delta}\coloneqq\varinjlim_{m}R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m},

where the transition maps
R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m^{\prime}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m}
are induced by the restriction morphisms
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m^{\prime}}
for
m^{\prime}\leq m
. Now
R^{i}\psi_{\delta}
has commuting left actions of
W_{F_{r}}
and
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
, and Proposition
1.10
.(iv) shows that

\displaystyle(R^{i}\psi_{\delta})^{1+\varpi^{m}\!\M_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}=R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m}.

Therefore
R^{i}\psi_{\delta}
is a
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times I_{F}
-admissible/continuous representation as in
[
20
, p. 24]
of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times W_{F_{r}}
over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
. Write
[R\psi_{\delta}]
for the virtual representation

\displaystyle[R\psi_{\delta}]\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{i}R^{i}\psi_{\delta}.

1.16.

At this point, we can finally begin to define the test function
f_{\tau,h}
mentioned in Theorem A. We shall start by defining
\phi_{\tau,h}
, which will end up being a transfer of
f_{\tau,h}
. Let
\tau
be an element of
W_{F}
with positive
v(\tau)
, write
r=v(\tau)
, and let
h
be a function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))
. We write
\phi_{\tau,h}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\GL_{n}(F_{r})\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}
for the function

\displaystyle\delta\mapsto\begin{cases}\tr(\tau\times h|[R\psi_{\delta}])&\mbox{if }\delta\mbox{ is in }\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r}),\\
0&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where we have identified
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
with
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Lemma
.

The map
\GL_{n}(\breve{F})\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\GL_{n}(\breve{F})
given by
g\mapsto g^{-1}\delta\sigma(g)
is open, where
\sigma
denotes the lift of
q
-Frobenius.

Proof.

This follows from reducing to the Lie algebra situation, using the Dieudonné–Manin classification to casework on
\delta
, and applying the nonarchimedean Banach open mapping theorem, c.f.
[
43
, Lemma 4.4]
.
∎

Lemma
1.16
implies that the subsets
B_{n,k}
of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})
are open. Because

\displaystyle\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})=\coprod_{k=1}^{n}B_{n,k},

we see that the subsets
B_{n,k}
are also closed.

One use Lemma
1.16
and proceed as in
[
43
, Proposition 4.3]
to show that
\phi_{\tau,h}
is locally constant. As
\phi_{\tau,h}
is supported on the compact subset
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})
by definition, we see that
\phi_{\tau,h}
is in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r}))
.

1.17.

Let
f_{\tau,h}
be a transfer of
\phi_{\tau,h}
, i.e. a function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F))
such that
f_{\tau,h}
and
\phi_{\tau,h}
have matching twisted orbital integrals
[
27
, (I.2.5, prop.)]
. We remark that while
f_{\tau,h}
is not uniquely determined as a function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F))
, its orbital integrals are well-defined by the fact that they equal the corresponding twisted orbital integrals of
\phi_{\tau,h}
. Hence the Weyl integration formula
[
27
, (II.2.10, formula (1)]
implies that the trace of
f_{\tau,h}
on admissible representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
is also well-defined.

Since we have now defined
f_{\tau,h}
, the statement of Theorem A makes sense.

2.
The first inductive lemma: trace identities and parabolic induction

In this section, we begin by recalling the
Bernstein–Zelevinsky classification
of irreducible smooth representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
, which we use to prove a lemma on checking equalities of traces. Then, we use Schneider–Zink’s theory of
tempered types
for
\GL_{n}(F)
, which is a version of Bushnell–Kutzko types for tempered representations, to construct test functions that pick out certain representations.

With these preliminaries, we proceed to the main result of this section: a lemma which allows us to prove Theorem A by inducting on
n
, provided that we verify certain conditions. The proof of our lemma uses theory of the
Bernstein center
, which describes the center of the category of smooth representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
. In subsequent sections, we will focus on verifying the necessary conditions for our inductive lemma.

2.1.

First, we recall Bernstein–Zelevinsky’s description of irreducible essentially
L^{2}
representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
. Let
\Delta
be a segment

\displaystyle\Delta=\{\pi_{0}[\textstyle\frac{1-m}{2}],\pi_{0}[\frac{3-m}{2}],\dotsc,\pi_{0}[\frac{m-1}{2}]\}

as in
[
49
, 3.1]
, where
m
is a positive divisor of
n
,
\pi_{0}
is an irreducible cuspidal representation of
\GL_{n/m}(F)
, and
[s]
denotes twisting by the unramified character
{\lvert\operatorname{det}\rvert}^{s}
. Consider the normalized parabolic induction

\displaystyle\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{0}[\textstyle\frac{1-m}{2}]\otimes\pi_{0}[\frac{3-m}{2}]\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{0}[\frac{m-1}{2}]),

where
P
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(m,\dotsc,m)
. This representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
has a unique irreducible quotient
[
49
, 9.1]
, which we denote by
Q(\Delta)
.

Recall that the irreducible essentially
L^{2}
representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
are those isomorphic to
Q(\Delta)

[
49
, 9.3]
. We see that
\Delta
is the cuspidal support of
Q(\Delta)
, and note that
Q(\Delta)
has unitary central character if and only if
\pi_{0}
does.

2.2.

Next, we proceed to arbitrary irreducible smooth representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
. Let
\{\Delta_{1},\dotsc,\Delta_{t}\}
be a collection of segments such that
\Delta_{i}
does not precede
\Delta_{j}
as in
[
49
, 4.1]
for
i<j
. Each
Q(\Delta_{i})
is an irreducible essentially
L^{2}
representation of
\GL_{n_{i}}(F)
, and we may form the normalized parabolic induction

\displaystyle\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}\left(Q(\Delta_{1})\otimes\dotsb\otimes Q(\Delta_{t})\right),

where
n=n_{1}+\dotsb+n_{t}
, and
P
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{t})
. When none of the
\Delta_{i}
are linked, this representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
is irreducible
[
49
, 9.7.(a)]
. For general
\Delta_{i}
, it has a unique irreducible quotient, which we denote by
Q(\Delta_{1},\dotsc,\Delta_{t})
. Furthermore, the induced representation above is irreducible precisely when
Q(\Delta_{1},\dotsc,\Delta_{t})
is a generic representation of
\GL_{n}(F)

[
49
, 9.7.(b)]
.

The isomorphism class of
Q(\Delta_{1},\dotsc,\Delta_{t})
does not depend on the ordering of the
\{\Delta_{1},\dotsc,\Delta_{t}\}
, as long as it still satisfies the condition that
\Delta_{i}
does not precede
\Delta_{j}
for
i<j
. Recall that every irreducible smooth representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
is isomorphic to
Q(\Delta_{1},\dotsc,\Delta_{t})
for a unique choice of
\{\Delta_{1},\dotsc,\Delta_{t}\}

[
49
, 9.7.(b)]
, up to reordering. We see that the multiset
\Delta_{1}\coprod\dotsb\coprod\Delta_{t}
is the cuspidal support of
Q(\Delta_{1},\dotsc,\Delta_{t})
. Furthermore, recall that
Q(\Delta_{1},\dotsc,\Delta_{t})
is tempered if and only if every
Q(\Delta_{i})
is
L^{2}

[
28
]
.

2.3.

The following terminology generalizes the Steinberg and trivial representations, respectively.

Definition
.

Let
t
be a positive divisor of
n
, and let
\pi_{0}
be an irreducible cuspidal representation of
\GL_{n/t}(F)
. We write
\St_{t}(\pi_{0})
for the representation

\displaystyle\St_{t}(\pi_{0})\coloneqq Q\left(\{\pi_{0}[\textstyle\frac{1-t}{2}],\pi_{0}[\frac{3-t}{2}],\dotsc,\pi_{0}[\frac{t-1}{2}]\}\right),

and we write
\Sp_{t}(\pi_{0})
for the representation

\displaystyle\Sp_{t}(\pi_{0})\coloneqq Q\left(\{\pi_{0}[\textstyle\frac{t-1}{2}]\},\{\pi_{0}[\frac{t-3}{2}]\},\dotsc,\{\pi_{0}[\frac{1-t}{2}]\}\right).

We say that a representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
is a
Steinberg module
if it is isomorphic to some
\Sp_{t}(\pi_{0})
for some
\pi_{0}
with unitary central character, and we say it is a
Speh module
5
5

5

What we call a Steinberg module is often called a generalized Steinberg representation. However, what we call a Speh module is actually less general than the usual definition of Speh representations.
if it is isomorphic to
\Sp_{t}(\pi_{0})
for some
\pi_{0}
with unitary central character. Note that
2.1
indicates that being a Steinberg module means the same thing as being irreducible
L^{2}
.

We recover the usual Steinberg and trivial representations by taking
t=n
and letting
\pi_{0}
be the trivial representation.

2.4.

We now turn to the following lemma on checking equalities of traces.

Lemma
.

Let
f
be a function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F))
, and suppose that
\tr(f|\pi)=0
whenever
\pi
is an irreducible smooth representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
that is

(a)

tempered but not
L^{2}
,

(b)

a Speh module.

Then
\tr(f|\pi)=0
for all irreducible smooth representations
\pi
of
\GL_{n}(F)
.

Proof.

Kazhdan’s density theorem
[
31
, Theorem 0]
6
6

6

The proof given here is stated for
p
-adic fields, but it only uses the Langlands classification and hence carries over to any nonarchimedean local field.
indicates that it suffices to check
\tr(f|\pi)=0
for all irreducible tempered
\pi
, and (a) above further reduces it to checking
\tr(f|\pi)=0
whenever
\pi
is irreducible
L^{2}
. Thus we can write
\pi
as
\St_{t}(\pi_{0})
, where
t
is some positive divisor of
n
, we set
d\coloneqq n/t
, and
\pi_{0}
is an irreducible cuspidal representation of
\GL_{d}(F)
with unitary central character.

If
t=1
, then
\pi=\pi_{0}=\Sp_{1}(\pi_{0})
, so (b) shows that
\tr(f|\pi)=0
. Next, suppose that
t\geq 2
. A routine calculation
[
20
, Lemma I.3.2]
using the graph-theoretic description of the Jordan–Hölder factors of

\displaystyle\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{0}[\textstyle\frac{1-t}{2}]\otimes\pi_{0}[\frac{3-t}{2}]\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{0}[\frac{t-1}{2}]),

where
P
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(d,\dotsb,d)
, yields the following equality of virtual smooth representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
:

\displaystyle\Sp_{t}(\pi_{0})+(-1)^{t}\pi=\sum_{j=1}^{t-1}(-1)^{j-1}\nInd_{P_{j}(L)}^{\GL_{n}(L)}\left(\Sp_{t-j}(\pi_{0}[\textstyle\frac{t-1+j}{2}])\otimes\St_{j}(\pi_{0}[\frac{j-1}{2}])\right),

where
P_{j}
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(d(t-j),dj)
. Note that
\Sp_{t}(\pi_{0})
is a Speh module. Therefore once we prove the following lemma, taking traces and using (b) yields the desired result.
∎

2.5 Lemma
.

Let
f
be a function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F))
, and suppose that
\tr(f|\pi)=0
whenever
\pi
is irreducible tempered but not
L^{2}
. Then for all partitions
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and irreducible smooth representations
\pi_{i}
of
\GL_{n_{i}}(F)
, we have

\displaystyle\tr\left(f|\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2})\right)=0,

where
P
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(n_{1},n_{2})
.

For any function
f
in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\GL_{n}(F))
, we write
f^{P,\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}
for its normalized
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
-invariant constant term along a parabolic subgroup
P
as in
[
37
, p. 80]
. It is a function in
C_{c}^{\infty}(M(F))
, where
M
is the standard Levi subgroup of
P
.

Proof of Lemma
2.5
.

Van Dijk’s formula
[
37
, Theorem 5.9]
implies that

\displaystyle\tr\left(f|\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2})\right)=\tr(f^{P,\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}|\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}).

Recall from
2.2
that, if
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are tempered, then

\displaystyle\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2})

is irreducible tempered but not
L^{2}
. In this situation, the left-hand side of the above equation vanishes by assumption. As
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
run over tempered representations of
\GL_{n_{1}}(F)
and
\GL_{n_{2}}(F)
, respectively,
\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}
runs over irreducible tempered representations of
M=\GL_{n_{1}}(F)\times\GL_{n_{2}}(F)
. Thus applying Kazhdan’s density theorem
[
31
, Theorem 0]
to
f^{P,\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}
indicates that the right-hand and hence left-hand side of the above equation vanishes for all
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
, as desired.
∎

2.6.

By using Schneider–Zink’s tempered types, we can construct the following test functions that pick out Speh modules.

Lemma
.

Let
\pi=\Sp_{t}(\pi_{0})
be a Speh module, where
t
is a positive divisor of
n
, and
\pi_{0}
is an irreducible cuspidal representation of
\GL_{n/t}(F)
with unitary central character. Then there exists a function
h
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))
such that

(i)

\tr(h|\pi)=1
,

(ii)

if
\pi^{\prime}
is an irreducible tempered representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
that is not isomorphic to
Q\left(\{\pi_{0}[s_{1}]\},\dotsc,\{\pi_{0}[s_{t}]\}\right)
for some purely imaginary
s_{1},\dotsc,s_{t}
, then
\tr(h|\pi^{\prime})=0
.

Proof.

We reduce this to tempered type theory as follows. Suppose we could find an irreducible smooth representation
\lambda
of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
such that

(i)

\pi
contains
\lambda
with multiplicity
1
,

(ii)

if
\pi^{\prime}
is an irreducible tempered representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
that is not isomorphic to
Q\left(\{\pi_{0}[s_{1}]\},\dotsc,\{\pi_{0}[s_{t}]\}\right)
for some purely imaginary
s_{1},\dotsc,s_{t}
, then
\pi^{\prime}
does not contain
\lambda
.

Then Schur orthogonality shows that the function
g\mapsto\overline{\tr(g|\lambda)}
yields the desired
h
. As for finding such a
\lambda
, we use
[
40
]
. More precisely, let
\lambda
be the representation
\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)
as in
[
40
, p. 30]
for
{\mathcal{P}}=t\cdot\Delta(\pi_{0},1)
, where we adopt the notation of
[
40
]
. Note that
\pi
lies in
\im Q_{\mathcal{P}}
, so (i) follows from
[
40
, Prop. 11, i.]
. Furthermore, we see that
{\mathcal{P}}
is a maximal partition-valued function on
{\mathcal{C}}
, so (ii) follows from
[
40
, Prop. 11, iii.]
.
∎

2.7.

Finally, we can introduce the main result of this section: our inductive lemma.

First Inductive Lemma
.

Assume that the following conditions hold for all admissible representations
\pi
of
\GL_{n}(F)
:

(a)

Theorem A is true for all
n^{\prime}<n
,

(b)

if
\pi
is isomorphic to the normalized parabolic induction

\displaystyle\nInd^{\GL_{n}(F)}_{P(F)}(\pi_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{t}),

where
t\geq 2
, the
\pi_{i}
are irreducible smooth representations of
\GL_{n_{i}}(F)
, we have
n=n_{1}+\dotsb+n_{t}
, and
P
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{t})
, then we have an equality of traces

\displaystyle\tr(f_{\tau,h}|\pi)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi_{1})(\textstyle\frac{n-n_{1}}{2})\oplus\dotsb\oplus\rho(\pi_{t})(\frac{n-n_{t}}{2})\right)\tr(h|\pi)

for all
\tau
in
W_{F}
with
v(\tau)>0
and
h
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))
,

(c)

if
\pi
is a Speh module or irreducible essentially
L^{2}
, there exists an
n
-dimensional
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\geq 0}
-virtual continuous representation
\rho(\pi)
of
W_{F}
satisfying the trace condition

\displaystyle\tr(f_{\tau,h}|\pi)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi)\right)\tr(h|\pi)

for all
\tau
in
W_{F}
with
v(\tau)>0
and
h
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))
,

(d)

if
\pi
is irreducible cuspidal, then the representation
\rho(\pi)
from condition (c) is actually a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-virtual continuous representation of
W_{F}
.

Then Theorem A is true for
n
.

Since semisimple representations of
W_{F}
are determined by their traces, any
\rho(\pi)
satisfying the equality of traces in Theorem A.(i) is unique up to isomorphism. In particular, the
\rho(\pi)
whose existence is posited by Theorem A.(i) is isomorphic to the
\rho(\pi)
whose existence is provided by condition (c).

We now proceed to prove the first inductive lemma. To this end, for the remainder of this section we shall assume that conditions (a)–(d) hold. Later, we shall verify these conditions in §
3
, §
4
, and §
8
.

2.8.

We begin with the following observation. Suppose that
\pi
is irreducible cuspidal. Then condition (c) provides an
n
-dimensional
{\mathbb{Q}}
-virtual continuous representation
\rho(\pi)
of
W_{F}
such that, when expanded in terms of the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-basis of irreducible continuous representations of
W_{F}
, every coefficient is non-negative. Condition (d) implies that these coefficients are integers, so
\rho(\pi)
corresponds to an actual
n
-dimensional semisimple continuous representation of
W_{F}
.

2.9.

Now let
\pi
be any irreducible smooth representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
. Before proceeding, we gather a few recollections on the Bernstein center. For any
f
in the Bernstein center
\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}
of
\GL_{n}(F)
, we write
\tr(f|\pi)
for the scalar by which
f
acts on
\pi
. We identify
\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}
with the product of the rings of regular functions on the Bernstein components of
\GL_{n}(F)

[
8
, 2.10]
.

Write
\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{t}
for the cuspidal support of
\pi
, where the
\pi_{i}
are irreducible cuspidal representations of
\GL_{n_{i}}(F)
such that
n=n_{1}+\dotsb+n_{t}
. Write
P
for the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{t})
. For any function
h
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F))
, van Dijk’s formula
[
37
, Theorem 5.9]
implies that the assignment

\displaystyle\pi\mapsto\tr\left(h|\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{t})\right)

yields an element of
\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}
, which we shall denote using
\widehat{h}
. When
\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{t})
is irreducible and hence isomorphic to
\pi
, we have
\tr(\widehat{h}|\pi)=\tr(h|\pi)
. However, we stress that this is false for general
\pi
.

2.10.

We construct an element
f_{\tau}
of
\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}
as follows. Write
r(\pi)
for the representation

\displaystyle r(\pi)\coloneqq\rho(\pi_{1})(\textstyle\frac{1-n_{1}}{2})\oplus\dotsb\oplus\rho(\pi_{t})(\frac{1-n_{t}}{2}),

which is well-defined for
t=1
by
2.8
and for
t\geq 2
by Theorem A.(i) for the
\pi_{i}
. Note that
r(\pi)
is semisimple. We shall see in Proposition
2.11
that
r(\pi)
is always isomorphic to
\rec(\pi)
, where the latter is defined as in Theorem A.

Proposition
.

The function
f_{\tau}
whose action on
\pi
is given by
\tr\left(\tau|\sigma(\pi)(\frac{n-1}{2})\right)
is an element of
\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}
.

This fulfills the desire to have a function-like object (in this case, an element of the Bernstein center, which is a distribution) associated with
\tau
whose trace equals the action of
\tau
on
r(\pi)
and hence on
\rec(\pi)
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
\pi\mapsto\tr\left(\tau|r(\pi)(\frac{n-1}{2})\right)
is a regular function on every Bernstein component, so we may focus on one particular
\pi
. First, use Schur orthogonality to obtain a function
h_{1}
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))
that satisfies

\displaystyle\tr\left(h_{1}|\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{t})\right)=1,

where
P
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{t})
. Now any other irreducible representation
\pi^{\prime}
in the Bernstein component of
\pi
is a subquotient of

\displaystyle\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}[s_{1}]\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{t}[s_{t}]),

where the
s_{i}
are complex numbers. The Iwasawa decomposition implies that
h_{1}^{P,\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}
is supported on
M({\mathcal{O}})
, where
M
is the standard Levi subgroup of
P
, so van Dijk’s formula
[
37
, Theorem 5.9]
shows that

\displaystyle\tr\left(h_{1}|\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}[s_{1}]\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{t}[s_{t}])\right)=\tr\left(h_{1}|\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{t})\right)=1.

Because
\pi_{1}[s_{1}],\dotsc,\pi_{t}[s_{t}]
is the cuspidal support of
\pi^{\prime}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tr\left(\tau|r(\pi^{\prime})(\textstyle\frac{n-1}{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi_{1}[s_{1}])(\textstyle\frac{n-n_{1}}{2})\oplus\dotsb\oplus\rho(\pi_{t}[s_{t}])(\frac{n-n_{t}}{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi_{1}[s_{1}])(\textstyle\frac{n-n_{1}}{2})\oplus\dotsb\oplus\rho(\pi_{t}[s_{t}])(\frac{n-n_{t}}{2})\right)\tr\left(h_{1}|\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}[s_{1}]\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{t}[s_{t}])\right).

This equals

\displaystyle\tr\left(f_{\tau,h_{1}}|\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}[s_{1}]\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{t}[s_{t}])\right)

for
t=1
by condition (c) and for
t\geq 2
by condition (b). Altogether, we see that
f_{\tau}
acts on the Bernstein component of
\pi
via
\widehat{f_{\tau,h_{1}}}
, so it is a regular function.
∎

2.11.

We can relate the distribution
f_{\tau}
to the test function
f_{\tau,h}
as follows. Recall that the convolution product
f_{\tau}*h
is a function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F))
.

Proposition
.

We have
\tr(f_{\tau,h}|\pi)=\tr(f_{\tau}*h|\pi)
.

Note that, since
f_{\tau}
lies in the Bernstein center of
\GL_{n}(F)
, we have

\displaystyle\tr(f_{\tau}*h|\pi)=\tr(f_{\tau}|\pi)\tr(h|\pi)=\tr\left(\tau|r(\pi)(\textstyle\frac{n-1}{2})\right)\tr(h|\pi).

By taking
\rho(\pi)=r(\pi)(\textstyle\frac{n-1}{2})
, we see that this proposition gives the existence part of Theorem A.(i) for
n
.

Proof of Proposition
2.11
.

By Lemma
2.4
, it suffices to consider
\pi
that are either Speh modules or irreducible tempered but not
L^{2}
. In the latter case, the descriptions given in
2.2
imply that
\pi
is isomorphic to a representation of the form considered in condition (b). Therefore condition (b) yields the desired result in this case.

Turning to the case where
\pi
is a Speh module, suppose that
\pi
is isomorphic to
\Sp_{t}(\pi_{0})
for some positive divisor
t
of
n
and irreducible cuspidal representation
\pi_{0}
of
\GL_{n/t}(F)
with unitary central character. If
t=1
, then
\pi=\pi_{0}
is cuspidal, and condition (c) gives the desired equality. Therefore suppose that
t\geq 2
, and let
h_{2}
be a function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))
as in Lemma
2.6
. We start by proving the case where
h=h_{2}
.

2.12 Lemma
.

For all irreducible smooth representations
\pi^{\prime}
of
\GL_{n}(F)
, we have
\tr\left(f_{\tau,h_{2}}|\pi^{\prime}\right)=\tr\left(f_{\tau}*h_{2}|\pi^{\prime}\right)
.

Proof.

Kazhdan’s density theorem
[
31
, Theorem 0]
indicates that it suffices to check this for irreducible tempered
\pi^{\prime}
. When
\pi^{\prime}
is not
L^{2}
, this identity is a case of Proposition
2.11
we proved above, so we now tackle the case when
\pi^{\prime}
is
L^{2}
. Since
t\geq 2
, the descriptions given in
2.1
and
2.2
indicate that
\pi^{\prime}
cannot be of the form
Q\left(\{\pi_{0}[s_{1}]\},\dotsc,\{\pi_{0}[s_{t}]\}\right)
for purely imaginary
s_{1},\dotsc,s_{t}
. Thus condition (c) and Lemma
2.6
.(ii) imply that

\displaystyle\tr\left(f_{\tau,h_{2}}|\pi^{\prime}\right)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi^{\prime})\right)\tr(h_{2}|\pi^{\prime})=0=\tr\left(\tau|r(\pi)(\textstyle\frac{n-1}{2})\right)\tr(h_{2}|\pi)=\tr\left(f_{\tau}*h_{2}|\pi^{\prime}\right),

which concludes the proof of Lemma
2.12
.
∎

Return to the proof of Proposition
2.11
. By plugging
\pi^{\prime}=\pi
into Lemma
2.12
and using condition (c) and Lemma
2.6
.(i), we see that

\displaystyle\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi)\right)=\tr\left(f_{\tau,h_{2}}|\pi\right)=\tr\left(f_{\tau}*h_{2}|\pi\right)=\tr\left(\tau|r(\pi)(\textstyle\frac{n-1}{2})\right).

Because semisimple representations of
W_{F}
are determined by their traces, this indicates that
\rho(\pi)
is isomorphic to
r(\pi)(\textstyle\frac{n-1}{2})
. In particular, applying condition (c) again verifies the desired equality when
\pi
is a Speh module.

∎

We conclude this section by finishing the proof of Lemma
2.7
, that is, by proving that Theorem A holds for
n
.

Proof of Lemma
2.7
.

We have already noted that Proposition
2.11
gives the existence part of Theorem A.(i) for
n
. Since semisimple representations of
W_{F}
are determined by their traces, any such
\rho(\pi)
satisfying the equality of traces in Theorem A.(i) is unique. As for Theorem A.(ii), it immediately follows from the associativity of normalized parabolic induction as well as the definition of
\sigma(\pi)
in terms of the cuspidal support of
\pi
.
∎

The next several sections will focus on verifying that conditions (b)–(d) hold, sometimes while assuming the inductive condition (a). More precisely, we shall prove condition (b) in Proposition
3.11
under the assumption that the inductive condition (a) holds, we will prove condition (d) in Proposition
4.11
while black-boxing condition (c), and we shall prove condition (c) itself in Proposition
8.13
. We will also prove the
n=1
case of Theorem A in Proposition
4.6
, which serves as the base case for applying Lemma
2.7
.

3.
Geometry of parabolic induction

Our goal in this section is to prove that condition (b) in Lemma
2.7
holds, under the assumption that the inductive condition (a) holds. To do this, we start by using étale local shtukas to prove the existence of transfers for
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
-conjugation-invariant functions in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))
. We then use work from this proof, along with our description of the deformation spaces
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
from §
1
, to prove an identity relating
\tr(f_{\tau,h}|\pi)
with the traces of
\phi_{\tau,h^{\prime}}
on certain Jacquet restrictions of
\pi
(where
h^{\prime}
now ranges over functions in
C^{\infty}_{c}(M({\mathcal{O}}))
for certain Levi blocks
M
in
\GL_{n}
). The proof of this identity uses
Casselman’s theorem
on characters of Jacquet modules. We finish the proof that condition (b) holds, under the assumption that the inductive condition (a) holds, by using the Bernstein–Zelevinsky
induction-restriction formula
to relate this aforementioned trace identity to a reformulated version of condition (b).

3.1.

We begin by using two descriptions of isomorphism classes of étale local shtukas over
\Spec\kappa_{r}
to prove the following bijection. In the end, this amounts to an application of completed unramified descent.

Lemma
.

The norm map
\delta\mapsto\delta\sigma(\delta)\dotsm\sigma^{r-1}(\delta)
induces a bijection

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\mbox{-}\sigma\mbox{-conjugacy classes in }\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\mbox{-conjugacy classes in }\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\}.

Proof.

We shall construct bijections between both the left-hand as well as right-hand sides and the set

\displaystyle\{\mbox{isomorphism classes of \'{e}tale local shtukas over }\Spec\kappa_{r}\mbox{ of rank }n\}

such that the composed bijection is equal to
\mathrm{N}
. On the left-hand side,
1.4
yields the desired bijection.

We now turn to the right-hand side. Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be an étale local shtuka over
\Spec\kappa_{r}
. Completed unramified descent implies that the isomorphism class of
{\mathscr{M}}
is determined by the isomorphism class of
\breve{\mathscr{M}}\coloneqq{\mathscr{M}}_{\overline{\kappa}}
along with a descent isomorphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sigma^{-r,*}\breve{\mathscr{M}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\breve{\mathscr{M}}
corresponding to the action of the geometric
q^{r}
-Frobenius map
\sigma^{-r}
. As
\breve{\mathscr{M}}
must be isomorphic to
(\overline{\kappa}[\![\varpi]\!]^{n},\sigma^{\oplus n})
by
1.6
, any such isomorphism identifies
\Aut(\breve{\mathscr{M}})
with
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
. Write
\breve{\mathscr{F}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sigma^{*}\breve{\mathscr{M}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{\mathscr{M}}
for the Frobenius of
\breve{\mathscr{M}}
, which is an isomorphism because
\breve{\mathscr{M}}
is étale, and write
\gamma
for the automorphism

\displaystyle\gamma\coloneqq\breve{\mathscr{F}}\circ\dotsb\circ\sigma^{r-1,*}\breve{\mathscr{F}}\circ\sigma^{r,*}f

of
\breve{\mathscr{M}}
, viewed as an element of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
. Since any other choice of isomorphism
\breve{\mathscr{M}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}(\overline{\kappa}[\![\varpi]\!]^{n},\sigma^{\oplus n})
preserves the
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
-conjugacy class of
\gamma
, this yields a bijection
\gamma\leftrightarrow{\mathscr{M}}
between
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
-conjugacy classes in
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
and isomorphism classes of étale local shtukas over
\Spec\kappa_{r}
of rank
n
.

Recall that
\breve{\mathscr{F}}
corresponds to
\delta\circ\sigma^{\oplus n}
under
1.4
. Since
f
corresponds to
(\sigma^{\oplus n})^{-r}
, we see that
\gamma
corresponds to

\displaystyle\underbrace{(\delta\circ\sigma^{\oplus n})\circ\dotsb\circ(\delta\circ\sigma^{\oplus n})}_{r\,\text{times}}\circ(\sigma^{\oplus n})^{-r}=\left(\delta\sigma(\delta)\dotsm\sigma^{r-1}(\delta)\right)\circ(\sigma^{\oplus n})^{r}\circ(\sigma^{\oplus n})^{-r}=\mathrm{N}\delta,

as desired.
∎

3.2.

We now construct certain functions with matching twisted orbital integrals, that is, certain transfers of functions. Let
h
be a
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
-conjugation-invariant function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))
, and form the function
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}
by sending
\delta\mapsto h(\mathrm{N}\delta)
. One can show that
\phi
is locally constant
[
42
, Corollary 2.3]
7
7

7

The proof given here is stated for mixed characteristic, but it works verbatim for any nonarchimedean field.
.

Lemma
.

The functions
h
and
\phi
have matching twisted orbital integrals.

Proof.

We need to prove that
\operatorname{O}_{\mathrm{N}\delta}(h)=\TO_{\delta,\sigma}(\phi)
for all regular
\delta
in
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})
, where
\operatorname{O}_{\mathrm{N}\delta}
and
\TO_{\delta,\sigma}
denote the orbital integral on
\mathrm{N}\delta
and twisted orbital integral on
\delta
, respectively, with respect to compatible Haar measures. We can prove this as follows: write
{\mathscr{M}}
for the étale local shtuka corresponding to
\delta
under
1.4
, and write
X
for the set of isomorphism classes of quasi-isogenies
\beta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathscr{M}}\dasharrow{\mathscr{M}}^{\prime}
between étale local shtukas. Note that the group
\Gamma
of self-quasi-isogenies
{\mathscr{M}}\dasharrow{\mathscr{M}}
has a left action on
X
given by sending
\beta
to
\beta\circ g^{-1}
for any
g
in
\Gamma
.

One can equate both
\operatorname{O}_{\mathrm{N}\delta}(h)
as well as
\TO_{\delta,\sigma}(\phi)
to the sum

\displaystyle\sum_{({\mathscr{M}}^{\prime},\beta)}\widetilde{h}({\mathscr{M}}^{\prime},\beta),

where
({\mathscr{M}}^{\prime},\beta)
ranges over elements in
\Gamma\backslash X
, and
\widetilde{h}
is the function sending
({\mathscr{M}}^{\prime},\beta)
to
h(\delta^{\prime})
, where
\delta^{\prime}
corresponds to
{\mathscr{M}}^{\prime}
under
1.4
. In a line of reasoning similar to the proof of Lemma
3.1
, we relate
\TO_{\delta,\sigma}(\phi)
to the above sum by using
1.4
, and we relate
\operatorname{O}_{\mathrm{N}\delta}(h)
to the above sum by using completed unramified descent, c.f.
[
44
, Proposition 4.3]

∎

3.3.

Next, we recast our results from §
1
in terms of our test functions. For this, recall that for any
\delta
in

\displaystyle\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r}),

we constructed an associated effective minuscule local shtuka
H_{\delta}
over
\Spec\kappa_{r}
of dimension
1
. Its connected–étale decomposition
[
21
, Proposition 2.9]
is
H_{\delta^{\circ}}\oplus H_{\delta^{\text{\'{e}t}}}
, where
H_{\delta^{\circ}}
has rank
k
, and
B_{n,k}
is the set of
\delta
such that
H_{\delta}
decomposes in this way. By considering
\delta
as an element of
\GL_{n}(\breve{\mathcal{O}})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}(\breve{\mathcal{O}})
, we obtain the pullback
\breve{H}_{\delta}
of
H_{\delta}
to
\Spec\overline{\kappa}
, and the space
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
parametrizes deformations of
\breve{H}_{\delta}
along with a Drinfeld level-
m
structure. We defined a virtual representation
[R\psi_{\delta}]
in terms of the cohomology of
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
as
m
varies, and we defined
\phi_{\tau,h}(\delta)
as a trace on
[R\psi_{\delta}]
.

Proposition
.

We have an equality

\displaystyle\phi_{\tau,h}(\delta)=\tr\left((\tau\times h)|\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\left([R\psi_{\delta^{\circ}}]\otimes C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}))\right)\right),

where
P_{k}
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(k,n-k)
,
\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}})
acts on
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}))
by left inverse multiplication, and
W_{F_{r}}
acts on
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}))
via the unramified action sending
\tau
to the action of right multiplication by
\mathrm{N}\delta^{\text{\'{e}t}}
.

Proof.

Let
f_{\sigma^{-r}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sigma^{-r,*}\breve{H}_{\delta}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\breve{H}_{\delta}
and
\varphi_{\sigma^{-r}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\sigma^{-r,*}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
be as in
1.13
. Using the Dieudonné equivalence
[
21
, Theorem 8.3]
to pass to
\varpi
-divisible local Anderson modules, the proof of Lemma
3.1
shows that the triple
(H^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}\circ f_{\sigma^{-r}})
is isomorphic to the triple
(H^{\prime},\mathrm{N}\delta^{\text{\'{e}t}}\circ\alpha^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
. Therefore
\varphi_{\sigma^{-r}}
sends
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,\alpha}
to
\sigma^{-r,^{*}}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,\mathrm{N}\delta^{\text{\'{e}t}}\circ\alpha}
. Taking cohomology of both sides in Proposition
1.12
and using
[
20
, Lemma I.5.6]
give us
