\displaystyle R\psi^{i}_{\delta,m}=\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})}\left(R\psi^{i}_{\delta^{\circ},m}\otimes\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}[\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})]\right),

where
W_{F_{r}}
acts on
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}[\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}})]
via the unramified action sending
\sigma^{-r}I_{F}
and hence
\tau
to the action of right multiplication by
N\delta^{\text{\'{e}t}}
. Taking the direct limit over
m
and forming the alternating sum over
i
gives an equality

\displaystyle[R\psi_{\delta}]=\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\left([R\psi_{\delta^{\circ}}]\otimes C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}))\right)

of virtual representations, where we have identified
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
with
{\mathbb{C}}
. Taking the trace of
\tau\times h
yields the desired result.
∎

3.4.

Before proceeding, we gather a few facts about twisted characters and automorphic base change. Recall that the norm map
\delta\mapsto\delta\sigma(\delta)\dotsm\sigma^{r-1}(\delta)
induces an injection

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\{\GL_{n}(F_{r})\mbox{-}\sigma\mbox{-conjugacy classes in }\GL_{n}(F_{r})\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}\{\GL_{n}(F_{r})\mbox{-conjugacy classes in }\GL_{n}(F_{r})\}

[
33
, Lemma 4.2]
. For any finite length smooth representation
\pi
of
\GL_{n}(F)
, its character distribution
\tr(-|\pi)
is represented by a locally integrable conjugation-invariant function
\Theta_{\pi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\GL_{n}(F)\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}

[
36
, Theorem (7.1)]
, which we call the
character
of
\pi
. Naturally, taking characters is additive. The function
\Theta_{\pi/F_{r}}^{\sigma}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\GL_{n}(F_{r})\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}
that sends
\delta\mapsto\Theta_{\pi}(\mathrm{N}\delta)
is locally integrable
[
27
, (II.2.8)]
, and we denote the associated distribution by
\tr\left((-,\sigma)|\pi/F_{r}\right)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F_{r}))\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}
.

The twisted Weyl integration formula
[
27
, (II.2.10, formula (1)]
implies that, if
f
and
\phi
are functions in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL(F))
and
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F_{r}))
, respectively, such that
f
and
\phi
have matching twisted orbital integrals, then we have

\displaystyle\tr\left((\phi,\sigma)|\pi/F_{r}\right)=\tr(f|\pi).

As suggested by our notation, if
\pi
is generic, then the twisted character of its base change lift
\pi_{F_{r}}
to
\GL_{n}(F_{r})
equals
\Theta_{\pi/F_{r}}^{\sigma}
, and we have
\tr((\phi,\sigma)|\pi_{F_{r}})=\tr\left((\phi,\sigma)|\pi/F_{r}\right)

[
27
, (II.2.8)]
.

3.5.

We use
3.4
to obtain the following compatibility result for
\rho
and unramified twists.

Proposition
.

Let
\pi
be an irreducible smooth representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
, and suppose that Theorem A.(i) holds for
\pi
. Then for any complex number
s
, Theorem A.(i) holds for
\pi[s]
. That is, there exists a unique
n
-dimensional semisimple continuous representation
\rho(\pi[s])
of
W_{F}
satisfying the following property:

\displaystyle\mbox{for all }\tau\mbox{ in }W_{F}\mbox{ with }v(\tau)>0\mbox{ and }h\mbox{ in }C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))\mbox{, we have }\tr\left(f_{\tau,h}|\pi[s]\right)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi[s])\right)\tr\left(h|\pi[s]\right).

This
\rho(\pi[s])
is given by
\rho(\pi[s])=\rho(\pi)(s)
.

Proof.

We immediately have
\tr\left(f_{\tau,h}|\pi[s]\right)=\tr\left((\phi_{\tau,h},\sigma)|\pi[s]/F_{r}\right)
by
3.4
. Unpacking definitions shows that the right hand side equals

\displaystyle\int_{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})}\!\dif\delta\,\tr\left(\tau\times h|[R\psi_{\delta}]\right)\Theta_{\pi[s]}(\mathrm{N}\delta).

Since
\pi[s]
is the twist of
\pi
by the character
{\lvert\operatorname{det}\rvert}^{s}
, we see that
\Theta_{\pi[s]}(\mathrm{N}\delta)=\Theta_{\pi}(\mathrm{N}\delta){\lvert\operatorname{det}\mathrm{N}\delta\rvert}^{s}
. As
\delta
lies in

\displaystyle\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r}),

its determinant has valuation
1
, which makes
\operatorname{det}\mathrm{N}\delta
have valuation
r
. Therefore the above integral becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q^{rs}}\int_{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})}\!\dif\delta\,\tr\left(\tau\times h|[R\psi_{\delta}]\right)\Theta_{\pi}(\mathrm{N}\delta)=\frac{1}{q^{rs}}\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi)\right)\tr\left(h|\pi\right)

by applying Theorem A.(i) for
\pi
. Because
\tau
acts through
(s)
via
1/q^{rs}
, the desired result follows.
∎

3.6.

In order to state the trace identity involving
f_{\tau,h}
and Jacquet restriction, we first need to recall the Peter–Weyl theorem for profinite groups
G
. One version of it says that we have a canonical isomorphism of smooth representations of
G\times G

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{c}(G)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\bigoplus_{\lambda}\End_{\mathbb{C}}(\lambda)=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\lambda^{\vee}\otimes\lambda

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto\bigoplus_{\lambda}\lambda(f),

where
\lambda
ranges over isomorphism classes of irreducible smooth representations of
G
, and
G\times G
acts on
C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
via letting
(g_{1},g_{2})
send
f
to the function
x\mapsto f(g_{2}^{-1}xg_{1})
. This implies, along with Schur orthogonality, that the trace map induces a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear bijection

\displaystyle\tr\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\{{\mathbb{C}}\mbox{-virtual admissible representations of }G\}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\{\mbox{conjugation-invariant distributions on }G\}.

We shall use this bijection to view conjugation-invariant distributions as
{\mathbb{C}}
-virtual admissible representations.

3.7.

We may now introduce an identity that relates the trace of
f_{\tau,h}
on
\pi
with traces on certain Jacquet restrictions of
\pi
. The proof shall critically use Proposition
3.3
, which itself is derived from the geometry of
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
as described in §
1
.

For all integers
1\leq k\leq n
, write
N_{k}
for the unipotent radical of
P_{k}
. Write
\pi_{N_{k}}
for the unnormalized Jacquet restriction of
\pi
, and suppose that we have an equality of virtual representations

\displaystyle\pi_{N_{k}}=\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}}\pi_{1,i}^{k}\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{k},

where
t_{k}
is a non-negative integer, and the
\pi_{1,i}^{k}
and
\pi_{2,i}^{k}
are finite length smooth representations of
\GL_{k}(F)
and
\GL_{n-k}(F)
, respectively. Note that we do not require the
\pi_{1,i}^{k}
and
\pi_{2,i}^{k}
to be irreducible. For any subset
X
, we denote the indicator function on that subset using
\mathbf{1}_{X}
.

Proposition
.

We have an equality of traces

\displaystyle\tr(f_{\tau,h}|\pi)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}q^{(n-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}}\tr\left(h|\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\left(\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\pi_{1,i}^{k}/F_{r})\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{k}\right)\right),

where we view the conjugation-invariant distribution
\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\pi_{1,i}^{k}/F_{r})
as a
{\mathbb{C}}
-virtual admissible representation of
\GL_{k}({\mathcal{O}})
via
3.6
.

In the proof, we take our Haar measures compatibly whenever possible.

Proof.

We immediately have
\tr(f_{\tau,h}|\pi)=\tr\left((\phi_{\tau,h},\sigma)|\pi/F_{r}\right)
. Because
\phi_{\tau,h}
is supported on

\displaystyle\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})=\coprod_{k=1}^{n}B_{n,k},

it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\tr\left((\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h},\sigma)|\pi/F_{r}\right)=q^{(n-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}}\tr\left(h|\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\left(\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\pi_{1,i}^{k}/F_{r})\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{k}\right)\right)

for all
k
. We begin with left-hand side. Applying the twisted Weyl integration formula
[
27
, (II.2.10, formula (1)]
to the function
\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h}
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F_{r}))
gives

\displaystyle\tr\left((\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h},\sigma)|\pi/F_{r}\right)=\sum_{T}\frac{1}{\#W(T,\GL_{n})}\int_{T(F_{r})^{\sigma-1}\backslash T(F_{r})}\!\dif\delta\,{\lvert D_{\GL_{n}}(\mathrm{N}\delta)\rvert}\TO_{\delta,\sigma}(\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h})\Theta_{\pi/F_{r}}^{\sigma}(\delta),

where
T
runs over conjugacy classes of maximal tori of
\GL_{n}
over
F
, and
D_{\GL_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\GL_{n}(F)\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}F
is the regular function defined by the equation

\displaystyle\operatorname{det}(t-\ad\gamma+1|{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{n}(F))\equiv D_{\GL_{n}}(\gamma)t^{n}\pmod{t^{n+1}}.

The decomposition
\delta=\delta^{\circ}\oplus\delta^{\text{\'{e}t}}
, along with the description of
\delta^{\circ}
and
\delta^{\text{\'{e}t}}
in
1.7
, shows that the only terms remaining in the above sum are

\displaystyle\sum_{T_{k}}\frac{1}{\#W(T_{k},\GL_{k})}\sum_{T_{n-k}}\frac{1}{\#W(T_{n-k},\GL_{n-k})}

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{(T_{k}(F_{r})^{\sigma-1}\backslash T_{k}(F_{r})_{1})\times(T_{n-k}(F_{r})^{\sigma-1}\backslash T_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}_{r}))}\!\dif\delta\,{\lvert D_{\GL_{n}}(\mathrm{N}\delta)\rvert}\TO_{\delta,\sigma}(\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h})\Theta_{\pi}(\mathrm{N}\delta),

where
T_{k}
runs over conjugacy classes of anisotropic mod center maximal tori of
\GL_{k}
,
T_{n-k}
runs over conjugacy classes of maximal tori of
\GL_{n-k}
, and we have
T=T_{k}\times T_{n-k}
.

With visible product decompositions beginning to form, let us make the change of variables
\delta=(\delta_{k},\delta_{n-k})
. Our goal is to break up the integrand in terms of
\GL_{k}
and
\GL_{n-k}
. By using block matrices to expand
{\lvert D_{\GL_{n}}(\mathrm{N}\delta)\rvert}
, we see that

\displaystyle{\lvert D_{\GL_{n}}(\mathrm{N}\delta)\rvert}=q^{(n-k)r}{\lvert D_{\GL_{k}}(\mathrm{N}\delta_{k})\rvert}\cdot{\lvert D_{\GL_{n-k}}(\mathrm{N}\delta_{n-k})\rvert}.

To obtain a similar decomposition of
\TO_{\delta,\sigma}(\mathbf{1}_{B_{b,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h})
, we shall use the following result.

3.8 Lemma
.

We have an equality of twisted orbital integrals

\displaystyle\TO_{\delta,\sigma}(\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h})=\TO_{(\delta_{k},\delta_{n-k}),\sigma}((\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h})|_{M_{k}(F_{r})}),

where
M_{k}
is the standard Levi subgroup of
P_{k}
,
(\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h})|_{M_{k}(F_{r})}
is the restriction of
\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h}
to
M_{k}(F_{r})
, and the right-hand side is the twisted orbital integral of
(\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h})|_{M_{k}(F_{r})}
on
(\delta_{k},\delta_{n-k})
.

Proof.

Using Nakayama’s lemma over
{\mathcal{O}}
to reduce to the residue field, we can explicitly calculate the normalized
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})
-
\sigma
-invariant constant term
(\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h})^{P_{k},\sigma,\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})}
of
\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h}
along
P_{k}
to be

\displaystyle(\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h})^{P_{k},\sigma,\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})}=q^{\frac{1}{2}(n-k)r}(\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h})|_{M_{k}(F_{r})}

[
42
, Lemma 6.6]
. The desired equality follows by applying a standard result
[
34
, (4.4.9)]
relating the twisted orbital integrals of
\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h}
and
(\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h})^{P_{k},\sigma,\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})}
.
∎

Return to the proof of Proposition
3.7
. Casselman’s theorem
[
11
, 5.2]
shows that

\displaystyle\Theta_{\pi}(\mathrm{N}\delta)=\Theta_{\pi_{N_{k}}}(\mathrm{N}\delta)=\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}}\Theta_{\pi_{1,i}^{k}}(\mathrm{N}\delta_{k})\Theta_{\pi_{2,i}^{k}}(\mathrm{N}\delta_{n-k}),

and by putting all of this together, we can rewrite our sum of integrals as

\displaystyle q^{(n-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}}\sum_{T_{k}}\frac{1}{\#W(T_{k},\GL_{k})}\int_{T_{k}(F_{r})^{\sigma-1}\backslash T_{k}(F_{r})_{1}}\!\dif\delta_{k}\,{\lvert D_{\GL_{k}}(\mathrm{N}\delta_{k})\rvert}\Theta_{\pi_{1,i}^{k}}(\mathrm{N}\delta_{k})\sum_{T_{n-k}}\frac{1}{\#W(T_{n-k},\GL_{n-k})}

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{T_{n-k}(F_{r})^{\sigma-1}\backslash T_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})}\!\dif\delta_{n-k}\,{\lvert D_{\GL_{n-k}}(\mathrm{N}\delta_{n-k})\rvert}\TO_{(\delta_{k},\delta_{n-k}),\sigma}(\phi_{\tau,h}|_{M_{k}(F_{r})})\Theta_{\pi_{2,i}^{k}}(\mathrm{N}\delta_{n-k}),

where we drop
\mathbf{1}_{B_{n,k}}
since our
\delta
all lie in
B_{n,k}
.

Before proceeding further, we apply Proposition
3.3
as follows. Write
\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},-)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}})\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}
for the function that sends

\displaystyle\gamma\mapsto\tr\left((\gamma,h)|\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\left(\phi_{\tau,-}(\delta_{k})\otimes C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}))\right)\right),

where we view the conjugation-invariant distribution
\phi_{\tau,-}(\delta_{k})
as a
{\mathbb{C}}
-virtual admissible representation of
\GL_{k}({\mathcal{O}})
via
3.6
,
\gamma
acts trivially on
\phi_{\tau,-}(\delta_{k})
, and
\gamma
acts on
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}))
via right multiplication. Note that, with Proposition
3.3
in mind,
\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}
heavily resembles
\phi_{\tau,h}
.

3.9 Lemma
.

The function
\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},-)
is in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n-k}({\mathcal{O}}))
, and it satisfies the equalities

\displaystyle\tr((\phi_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},-),\sigma)|\pi_{2,i}^{k}/F_{r})=\tr(\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},-)|\pi_{2,i}^{k})=\tr\left(h|\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}(\phi_{\tau,-}(\delta_{k})\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{k})\right).

Proof.

Proposition
3.3
indicates that
\phi_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},\delta)=\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},\mathrm{N}\delta)
. Thus
3.2
reduces the first claim to the statement that
\phi_{\tau,h}
is in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r}))
, which holds by
1.16
. For the second claim, note that Lemma
3.2
implies
\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},-)
and
\phi_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},-)
have matching twisted orbital integrals. Therefore
3.4
yields
\tr((\phi_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},-),\sigma)|\pi_{2,i}^{k}/F_{r})=\tr(\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},-)|\pi_{2,i}^{k})
.

From here, applying the Peter–Weyl theorem and Schur orthogonality to the definition of
\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},-)
show that
\tr(\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}(\delta_{k},-)|\pi_{2,i}^{k})=\tr\left(h|\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}(\phi_{\tau,-}(\delta_{k})\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{k})\right)
.
∎

Return to the proof of Proposition
3.7
. Applying the twisted Weyl integration formula
[
27
, (II.2.10, formula (1)]
to the function
\TO_{\delta_{k},\sigma}(\phi_{\tau,h}(-,-))
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n-k}(F_{r}))
allows us to collapse the sum over
T_{n-k}
and simplify our expression into

\displaystyle q^{(n-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}}\sum_{T_{k}}\frac{1}{\#W(T_{k},\GL_{k})}\int_{T_{k}(F_{r})^{\sigma-1}\backslash T_{k}(F_{r})_{1}}\!\dif\delta_{k}\,{\lvert D_{\GL_{k}}(\mathrm{N}\delta_{k})\rvert}\Theta_{\pi_{1,i}^{k}}(\mathrm{N}\delta_{k})\TO_{\delta_{k},\sigma}(\tr(\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}(-,-)|\pi_{2,i}^{k})),

where we first used
\tr((\TO_{\delta_{k},\sigma}(\phi_{\tau,h}(-,-)),\sigma)|\pi_{2,i}^{k}/F_{r})=\TO_{\delta_{k},\sigma}(\tr((\phi_{\tau,h}(-,-),\sigma)|\pi_{2,i}^{k}/F_{r}))
and then the first equality in Lemma
3.9
. Applying the twisted Weyl integration formula to the function
\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\tr(\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}(-,-)|\pi_{2,i}^{k})
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{k}(F_{r}))
lets us collapse the sum over
T_{k}
, and we get

\displaystyle q^{(n-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}}\tr\left((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\tr(\widetilde{f}_{\tau,h}(-,-)|\pi_{2,i}^{k}),\sigma)|\pi_{1,i}^{k}/F_{r})\right).

Now the second equality in Lemma
3.9
turns this into

\displaystyle q^{(n-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}}\tr\left(h|\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\left(\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\pi_{1,i}^{k}/F_{r})\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{k}\right)\right),

which completes the proof of Proposition
3.7
.
∎

3.10.

Before moving on to the proof of condition (b) in Lemma
2.7
, we record a useful consequence of van Djik’s formula. Let
P
be a proper standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
, write
M
for the standard Levi subgroup of
P
, let
\pi^{\prime}
be an irreducible smooth representation of
M(F)
, and write
\pi
for the induced representation

\displaystyle\pi\coloneqq\nInd_{M(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}\pi^{\prime}.

As
\pi^{\prime}
has finite length, so does
\pi
.

Lemma
.

Let
\phi
be a function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F_{r}))
that is supported in elements of
\GL_{n}(F_{r})
whose norm is elliptic. Then we have
\tr((\phi,\sigma)|\pi/F_{r})=0
.

Proof.

Let
f
be a transfer of
\phi
, i.e. a function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}(F))
such that
f
and
\phi
have matching twisted orbital integrals. Then
f
is supported in elliptic elements of
\GL_{n}(F)
, and no such element is contained in
P(F)
because
P
is a proper parabolic subgroup. Therefore the normalized
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
-invariant constant term
f^{P,\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}
of
f
along
P
is identically zero on
M(F)
. Our remark from
3.4
and van Djik’s formula
[
37
, Theorem 5.9]
together yield

\displaystyle\tr((\phi,\sigma)|\pi/F_{r})=\tr(f|\pi)=\tr(f^{P,\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}|\pi^{\prime})=0,

as desired.
∎

We will apply the above lemma to
\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,h}
in the proof of Proposition
3.11
.

3.11.

Finally, we use Proposition
3.7
to prove condition (b), assuming condition (a).

Proposition
.

Assume that the inductive condition (a) in Lemma
2.7
holds, that is, Theorem A is true for
n^{\prime}<n
. Let
\pi
be a smooth representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
, and suppose it is of the form

\displaystyle\pi=\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\pi_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes\pi_{t}),

where
t\geq 2
, the
\pi_{i}
are some irreducible smooth representations of
\GL_{n_{i}}(F)
with
n=n_{1}+\dotsb+n_{t}
, and
P
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{t})
. Then we have an equality of traces

\displaystyle\tr(f_{\tau,h}|\pi)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi_{1})(\textstyle\frac{n-n_{1}}{2})\oplus\dotsb\oplus\rho(\pi_{t})(\frac{n-n_{t}}{2})\right)\tr(h|\pi)

for all
\tau
in
W_{F}
with
v(\tau)>0
and
h
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))
.

In the proof of Proposition
3.11
, we shall frequently pass between normalized and un-normalized induction. For this, one immediately checks that

\displaystyle\nInd_{Q(F)}^{\GL_{m}(F)}(\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{2})=\Ind_{Q(F)}^{\GL_{m}(F)}(\lambda_{1}[\textstyle\frac{m_{2}}{2}]\otimes\lambda_{2}[-\frac{m_{1}}{2}]),

where the
\lambda_{i}
are irreducible smooth representations of
\GL_{m_{i}}(F)
with
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
, and
Q
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{m}
with block sizes
(m_{1},m_{2})
.

We also recall that the two representations

\displaystyle\nInd_{Q(F)}^{\GL_{m}(F)}(\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{2})\mbox{ and }\nInd_{Q^{\prime}(F)}^{\GL_{m}(F)}(\lambda_{2}\otimes\lambda_{1}),

where
Q^{\prime}
denotes the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(m_{2},m_{1})
, have the same Jorder–Hölder series
[
9
, 2.9]
. This enables us to identify them as virtual representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
.

Proof of Proposition
3.11
.

We start with some immediate reductions. By using the transitivity of parabolic induction
[
49
, 1.7]
, it suffices to prove the proposition for
t=2
. Furthermore, because swapping
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
leaves the virtual representation
\pi
unchanged, we may assume that
n_{1}\leq n_{2}
. Finally, by taking the trace of
h
afterwards, Proposition
3.7
shows that it suffices to find, for all integers
1\leq k\leq n
, a decomposition of virtual representations

\displaystyle\pi_{N_{k}}=\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}}\pi_{1,i}^{k}\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{k}

of
\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F)
, where the
\pi_{1,i}^{k}
and
\pi_{2,i}^{k}
have finite length, such that we have an equality of
{\mathbb{C}}
-virtual representations

(
\star
)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}q^{(n-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}}\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\left(\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\pi_{1,i}^{k}/F_{r})\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{k}\right)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi_{1})(\textstyle\frac{n-n_{1}}{2})\oplus\rho(\pi_{2})(\frac{n-n_{2}}{2})\right)\pi

of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
. We will choose these
\pi_{1,i}^{k}
and
\pi_{2,i}^{k}
in a way that relates them to
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
. To do so, we shall use the following
induction-restriction formula
of Bernstein–Zelevinsky.

3.12.

Write
T
for the standard maximal torus of
\GL_{n}
, and write
B
for the standard Borel subgroup of
\GL_{n}
. Let
P
and
Q
be standard parabolic subgroups of
\GL_{n}
, and denote their standard Levi subgroups using
M
and
N
, respectively. We identify the Weyl group
W(T,\GL_{n})
with the symmetric group
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
acting by permuting entries. Write
W_{M,N}
for the subset

\displaystyle W_{M,N}\coloneqq\{w\in W(T,\GL_{n})|w(M\cap B)\subseteq B\mbox{ and }w^{-1}(N\cap B)\subseteq B\}

of
W(T,\GL_{n})
.

Lemma

(
[
9
, 2.12]
)
.

Let
\pi
be an irreducible smooth representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
. Then we have an equality of virtual representations

\displaystyle\nRes_{Q(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(\nInd_{P(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}\pi)=\sum_{w}\nInd_{Q^{\prime}(F)}^{N(F)}(w(\nRes_{P^{\prime}(F)}^{M(F)}\pi))

of
N(L)
, where
\nRes
denotes normalized Jacquet restriction,
w
runs over all elements of
W_{M,N}
,
P^{\prime}
denotes the standard parabolic subgroup of
M
whose standard Levi subgroup is
M\cap w^{-1}(N)
, and
Q^{\prime}
denotes the standard parabolic subgroup of
N
whose standard Levi subgroup is
w(M)\cap N
.

Return to the proof of Proposition
3.11
. Our goal will be to apply
\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|-/F_{r})
to the
\pi_{1,i}^{k}
. At this point, Lemma
3.10
indicates that the terms corresponding to properly parabolically induced
\pi_{1,i}^{k}
will vanish. Therefore, when using the induction-restriction formula for
M=\GL_{n_{1}}\times\GL_{n_{2}}
and
N=\GL_{k}\times\GL_{n-k}
, we will only be interested in the terms for which
w(M)\cap N
contains
\GL_{k}
.

With these choices of
M
and
N
, now
W_{M,N}
corresponds precisely to the set of permutations
w
in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
for which

•

w
is order-preserving on
\{1,\dotsc,n_{1}\}
and
\{n_{1}+1,\dotsc,n\}
,

•

w^{-1}
is order-preserving on
\{1,\dotsc,k\}
and
\{k+1,\dotsc,n\}
.

Furthermore, the
w(M)\cap N\supseteq\GL_{k}
condition is equivalent to asking that

\displaystyle w(\{1,\dotsc,n_{1}\})\supseteq\{1,\dotsc,k\}\mbox{ or }w(\{n_{1}+1,\dotsc,n\})\supseteq\{1,\dotsc,k\}.

If
k\leq n_{1}
, then the only such
w
in
W_{M,N}
are the identity permutation and the permutation
\theta
that sends
\{1,\dotsc,n_{1}\}
to
\{n_{2}+1,\dotsc,n\}
and
\{n_{1}+1,\dotsc,n\}
to
\{1,\dotsc,n_{2}\}
while preserving their internal orders. If instead
n_{1}<k\leq n_{2}
, then the identity no longer satisfies
w(M)\cap N\supseteq\GL_{k}
, although
\theta
continues to do so. Finally, for
n_{2}<k
, no element of
W_{M,N}
has
w(M)\cap N\supseteq\GL_{k}
. Altogether, Lemma
3.12
yields an equality

\displaystyle\pi_{N_{k}}=

\displaystyle\Ind_{P_{n_{1}}(F)\cap(\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F))}^{\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F)}(\pi^{\prime}_{N_{k_{1}}}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime})

(5)

\displaystyle+\Ind_{P_{n_{2}}(F)\cap(\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F))}^{\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F)}(\pi^{\prime\prime}_{N_{k_{2}}}[n_{1}]\otimes\pi^{\prime}[-n_{2}])+\sum_{j=1}^{u_{k}}\tau_{1,j}^{k}\otimes\tau_{2,j}^{k}

of virtual representations of
\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F)
, where
\pi^{\prime}
denotes
\pi_{1}[\frac{n_{2}}{2}]
,
\pi^{\prime\prime}
denotes
\pi_{2}[-\frac{n_{1}}{2}]
,
N_{k_{1}}
denotes the unipotent radical of
P_{n_{1}}\cap\GL_{k}
,
N_{k_{2}}
denotes the unipotent radical of
P_{n_{2}}\cap\GL_{k}
, and the
\tau_{1,1}^{k},\dotsc,\tau_{1,u_{k}}^{k}
(respectively
\tau_{2,1}^{k},\dotsc,\tau_{2,u_{k}}^{k}
) are smooth representations of
\GL_{k}(F)
(respectively
\GL_{n-k}(F)
) such that the
\tau_{1,j}^{k}
are properly induced representations of
\GL_{k}(F)
. As remarked above, if
k>n_{1}
or
k>n_{2}
, then we can absorb the first or second term on the right hand side into the third summation term, respectively.

So suppose that
k\leq n_{1}
and
k\leq n_{2}
. We shall begin by studying the first term on the right hand side of Equation (
3.12
). Because the unnormalized Jacquet restriction
\pi^{\prime}_{N_{k_{1}}}
of
\pi^{\prime}
is a smooth representation of
\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n_{1}-k}(F)
of finite length, we can write its virtual representation as

\displaystyle\pi^{\prime}_{N_{k_{1}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime}}\pi^{\prime k}_{1,i}\otimes\pi^{\prime k}_{2,i},

where
t_{k}^{\prime}
is a non-negative integer, and the
\pi^{\prime k}_{1,i}
and
\pi^{\prime k}_{2,i}
are irreducible smooth representations of
\GL_{k}(F)
and
\GL_{n_{1}-k}(F)
, respectively. Write
P_{k,n_{1}}
for the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(k,n_{1}-k,n_{2})
. By applying
\Ind_{P_{n_{1}}(F)\cap(\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F))}^{\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F)}(-\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime})
to the above decomposition of
\pi^{\prime}_{N_{k_{1}}}
, we obtain the equality of virtual representations

(6)

\displaystyle\Ind_{P_{n_{1}}(F)\cap(\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F))}^{\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F)}(\pi^{\prime}_{N_{k_{1}}}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime}}\pi_{1,i}^{\prime k}\otimes\Ind_{P_{k,n_{1}}(F)\cap\GL_{n-k}(F)}^{\GL_{n-k}(F)}(\pi_{2,i}^{\prime k}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}),

giving an alternate description of the first term on the right hand side of Equation (
3.12
).

Now Proposition
3.7
for
\pi^{\prime}
indicates that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n_{1}}q^{(n_{1}-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime}}\tr\left(h_{1}|\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})\cap\GL_{n_{1}}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n_{1}}({\mathcal{O}})}\left(\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\BC(\pi_{1,i}^{\prime k}))\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{\prime k}\right)\right)=\tr(f_{\tau,h_{1}}|\pi^{\prime})

for all functions
h_{1}
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n_{1}}({\mathcal{O}}))
. Theorem A.(i) for
\pi^{\prime}
implies that
\tr(f_{\tau,h_{1}}|\pi^{\prime})=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi^{\prime})\right)\tr(h_{1}|\pi^{\prime})
, so
3.6
yields an equality of
{\mathbb{C}}
-virtual representations

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n_{1}}q^{(n_{1}-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime}}\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})\cap\GL_{n_{1}}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n_{1}}({\mathcal{O}})}\left(\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\BC(\pi_{1,i}^{\prime k}))\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{\prime k}\right)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi^{\prime})\right)\pi^{\prime}

of
\GL_{n_{1}}({\mathcal{O}})
. Since we defined
\pi^{\prime}
to be
\pi_{1}[\textstyle\frac{n_{2}}{2}]
, taking
\Ind_{P_{n_{1}}(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}(-\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime})
on both sides shows that

(7)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n_{1}}q^{(n-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime}}\Ind_{P_{k,n_{1}}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\left(\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\BC(\pi_{1,i}^{\prime k}))\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{\prime k}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}\right)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi_{1})(\textstyle\frac{n_{2}}{2})\right)\pi

as
{\mathbb{C}}
-virtual representations of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
, where we used Proposition
3.5
to identify
\rho(\pi^{\prime})
with
\rho(\pi_{1})(\frac{n_{2}}{2})
.

Next, we turn to the second term on the right hand side of Equation (
3.12
). As with the first term, we have an equality of virtual representations

\displaystyle\pi^{\prime\prime}_{N_{k_{2}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\pi^{\prime\prime k}_{1,i}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime k}_{2,i},

where
t_{k}^{\prime\prime}
is a non-negative integer, and the
\pi^{\prime\prime k}_{1,i}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime k}_{2,i}
are irreducible smooth representations of
\GL_{k}(F)
and
\GL_{n_{2}-k}(F)
, respectively. Write
P_{k,n_{2}}
for the standard parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
with block sizes
(k,n_{2}-k,n_{1})
. By applying
\Ind_{P_{n_{2}}(F)\cap(\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F))}^{\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F)}((-)[n_{1}]\otimes\pi^{\prime}[-n_{2}])
to the above decomposition of
\pi^{\prime\prime}_{N_{k_{2}}}
, we obtain the equality of virtual representations

(8)

\displaystyle\Ind_{P_{n_{2}}(F)\cap(\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F))}^{\GL_{k}(F)\times\GL_{n-k}(F)}(\pi^{\prime\prime}_{N_{k_{2}}}[n_{1}]\otimes\pi^{\prime}[-n_{2}])=\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\pi_{1,i}^{\prime\prime k}[n_{1}]\otimes\Ind_{P_{k,n_{2}}(F)\cap\GL_{n-k}(F)}^{\GL_{n-k}(F)}(\pi_{2,i}^{\prime\prime k}[n_{1}]\otimes\pi^{\prime}[-n_{2}]),

giving an alternate description of the second term on the right hand side of Equation (
3.12
).

Now Proposition
3.7
for
\pi^{\prime\prime}
indicates that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n_{2}}q^{(n_{2}-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\tr\left(h_{2}|\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})\cap\GL_{n_{2}}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n_{2}}({\mathcal{O}})}\left(\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\BC(\pi_{1,i}^{\prime\prime k}))\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{\prime\prime k}\right)\right)=\tr(f_{\tau,h_{2}}|\pi^{\prime\prime})

for all functions
h_{2}
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n_{2}}({\mathcal{O}}))
. Theorem A.(i) for
\pi^{\prime\prime}
implies that
\tr(f_{\tau,h_{2}}|\pi^{\prime\prime})=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi^{\prime\prime})\right)\tr(h_{2}|\pi^{\prime\prime})
, so
3.6
yields an equality of
{\mathbb{C}}
-virtual representations

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n_{2}}q^{(n_{2}-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\Ind_{P_{k}({\mathcal{O}})\cap\GL_{n_{2}}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n_{2}}({\mathcal{O}})}\left(\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\BC(\pi_{1,i}^{\prime\prime k}))\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{\prime\prime k}\right)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi^{\prime\prime})\right)\pi^{\prime\prime}

of
\GL_{n_{2}}({\mathcal{O}})
. Since we defined
\pi^{\prime\prime}
to be
\pi_{2}[-\textstyle\frac{n_{1}}{2}]
, taking
\Ind_{P_{n_{2}}(F)}^{\GL_{n}(F)}((-)[n_{1}]\otimes\pi^{\prime}[-n_{2}])
on both sides shows that

(9)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n_{2}}q^{(n_{2}-k)r}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\Ind_{P_{k,n_{2}}({\mathcal{O}})}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\left(\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\BC(\pi_{1,i}^{\prime\prime k}[n_{1}]))\otimes\pi_{2,i}^{\prime\prime k}[n_{1}]\otimes\pi^{\prime}[-n_{2}]\right)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\pi_{2})(\textstyle\frac{n_{1}}{2})\right)\pi

as
{\mathbb{C}}
-virtual representations of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
, where we used Proposition
3.5
to identify
\rho(\pi^{\prime\prime})
with
\rho(\pi_{2})(-\frac{n_{2}}{2})
.

We piece together the above work as follows. Equation (
3.12
), Equation (
6
), and Equation (
8
) indicate that

\displaystyle\pi_{N_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime}}\pi_{1,i}^{\prime k}\otimes\Ind_{P_{k,n_{1}}(F)\cap\GL_{n-k}(F)}^{\GL_{n-k}(F)}(\pi_{2,i}^{\prime k}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{t_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\pi_{1,i}^{\prime\prime k}[n_{1}]\otimes\Ind_{P_{k,n_{2}}(F)\cap\GL_{n-k}(F)}^{\GL_{n-k}(F)}(\pi_{2,i}^{\prime\prime k}[n_{1}]\otimes\pi^{\prime}[-n_{2}])+\sum_{j=1}^{u_{k}}\tau_{1,j}^{k}\otimes\tau_{2,j}^{k},

and we take this decomposition for our
\pi^{k}_{1,i}
and
\pi^{k}_{2,i}
. Because the
\tau_{1,j}^{k}
are properly induced, Lemma
3.10
shows that the
\tr((\mathbf{1}_{B_{k}}\cdot\phi_{\tau,-},\sigma)|\BC(\tau_{1,j}^{k}))
terms vanish. Therefore the sum of Equation (
7
) and Equation (
9
) is precisely Equation (

⋆

‣
3.11
), and this concludes the proof of Proposition
3.11
.
∎

4.
The Lubin–Tate tower

In this section, our goal is to prove that condition (d) in Lemma
2.7
holds, under the assumption that condition (c) holds. Although we postpone the proof of condition (c) for later, our proof of condition (d) does not use anything from the construction of condition (c)—we may safely black box the latter. Along the way, we also prove the
n=1
case of Theorem A, which serves as the base case for applying Lemma
2.7
.

We begin by using connected local shtukas to prove, in the same vein as Lemma
3.1
and Lemma
3.2
, the existence of transfers for certain conjugation-invariant functions on division algebras over
F
. We then introduce the
Lubin–Tate tower
, which is closely related to our deformation spaces from §
1
. By studying this relation, along with the fact that it gives the Lubin–Tate proof of local class field theory, we prove the
n=1
case of Theorem A. We also use this relation to compare the cohomologies of the Lubin–Tate tower and
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
. This comparison is stated in terms of the
local Jacquet–Langlands correspondence
, which relates representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
with representations of division algebras. Finally, we use results from Jacquet–Piatetski-Shapiro–Shalika’s theory of
new-vectors
, which are certain elements in irreducible generic representations of
\GL_{n}(F)
, to conclude the proof of condition (d).

4.1.

We start by using two descriptions of isomorphism classes of connected local shtukas over
\Spec\kappa_{r}
to prove the following proposition. Write
B
for the central division algebra over
F
of Hasse invariant
\frac{1}{n}
, and write
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
for its ring of integers. By abuse of notation, we denote the normalized valuation on
B
using
v
. Write
B_{r}
for the subset of
b
in
B
satisfying
v(b)=r
.

Lemma
.

There exists a bijection

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\mbox{-}\sigma\mbox{-conjugacy classes in }B_{n}\}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{\times}\mbox{-conjugacy classes in }B_{r}\}

such that the characteristic polynomial of
\mathrm{N}\delta
equals that of
\delta\sigma(\delta)\dotsm\sigma^{r-1}(\delta)
. By abuse of notation, we call this the
norm map
.

This result and its proof are entirely analogous to those of Lemma
3.1
.

Proof.

We shall construct bijections between both the left-hand as well as right-hand sides and the set

\displaystyle\{\mbox{isomorphism classes of connected local shtukas over }\Spec\kappa_{r}\mbox{ of rank }n\},

and we denote the composed bijection using
\mathrm{N}
. On the left-hand side,
1.4
yields the desired bijection.

We now turn to the right-hand side. Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be a connected local shtuka over
\Spec\kappa_{r}
. Completed unramified descent implies that the isomorphism class of
{\mathscr{M}}
is determined by the isomorphism class of
\breve{\mathscr{M}}\coloneqq{\mathscr{M}}_{\overline{\kappa}}
along with a descent isomorphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sigma^{-r,*}\breve{\mathscr{M}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\breve{\mathscr{M}}
corresponding to the action of the geometric
q^{r}
-Frobenius map
\sigma^{-r}
. As
\breve{\mathscr{M}}
must be isomorphic to the local shtuka associated with the Lubin–Tate module, after choosing such an isomorphism we may identify
\End(\breve{\mathscr{M}})
with
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
. Write
\breve{\mathscr{F}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sigma^{*}\breve{\mathscr{M}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{\mathscr{M}}
for the Frobenius of
\breve{\mathscr{M}}
, which corresponds to an element of valuation
1
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
, and write
\gamma
for the endomorphism

\displaystyle\gamma\coloneqq\breve{\mathscr{F}}\circ\dotsb\circ\sigma^{r-1,*}\breve{\mathscr{F}}\circ\sigma^{r,*}f

of
\breve{\mathscr{M}}
, viewed as an element of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
. As
\sigma^{r,*}f
is an isomorphism and hence corresponds to an element of valuation
0
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
, this shows that
v(\gamma)=r
. Since any other choice of isomorphism between
\breve{\mathscr{M}}
and the local shtuka associated with the Lubin–Tate module preserves the
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{\times}
-conjugacy class of
\gamma
, this yields a bijection
\gamma\leftrightarrow{\mathscr{M}}
between
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{\times}
-conjugacy classes in
B_{r}
and isomorphism classes of connected local shtukas over
\Spec\kappa_{r}
of rank
n
.

Recall that
\breve{\mathscr{F}}
corresponds to
\delta\circ\sigma^{\oplus n}
under
1.4
. Since
f
corresponds to
(\sigma^{\oplus n})^{-r}
, we see that
\gamma
corresponds to

\displaystyle\underbrace{(\delta\circ\sigma^{\oplus n})\circ\dotsb\circ(\delta\circ\sigma^{\oplus n})}_{r\,\text{times}}\circ(\sigma^{\oplus n})^{-r}=\left(\delta\sigma(\delta)\dotsm\sigma^{r-1}(\delta)\right)\circ(\sigma^{\oplus n})^{r}\circ(\sigma^{\oplus n})^{-r}=\delta\sigma(\delta)\dotsm\sigma^{r-1}(\delta),

as desired.
∎

4.2.

We will now construct certain functions with matching twisted orbital integrals, that is, certain transfers of functions. Let
h
be an
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{\times}
-conjugation-invariant function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{r})
, and form the function
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{n}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}
by sending
\delta\mapsto h(\mathrm{N}\delta)
. One can show that
\phi
is locally constant
[
42
, Corollary 2.3]
8
8

8

The proof given here is stated for mixed characteristic and
\GL_{n}
, but it only uses Lang’s lemma and the relationship between inner twists of
\GL_{n}
and central division algebras. In particular, it adapts to equal characteristic and any inner twist of
\GL_{n}
.
.

Lemma
.

The functions
h
and
\phi
have matching twisted orbital integrals.

This result and its proof are entirely analogous to those of Lemma
3.2
.

Proof.

We need to prove that
\operatorname{O}_{\mathrm{N}\delta}(h)=\TO_{\delta,\sigma}(\phi)
for all regular
\delta
in
B_{n}
, where
\operatorname{O}_{\mathrm{N}\delta}
and
\TO_{\delta,\sigma}
denote the orbital integral on
\mathrm{N}\delta
and twisted orbital integral on
\delta
, respectively, with respect to compatible Haar measures. We can prove this as follows: write
{\mathscr{M}}
for the connected local shtuka corresponding to
\delta
under
1.4
, and write
X
for the set of isomorphism classes of quasi-isogenies
\beta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathscr{M}}\dasharrow{\mathscr{M}}^{\prime}
between connected local shtukas. Note that the group
\Gamma
of self-quasi-isogenies
{\mathscr{M}}\dasharrow{\mathscr{M}}
has a left action on
X
given by sending
\beta
to
\beta\circ g^{-1}
for any
g
in
\Gamma
.

One can equate both
\operatorname{O}_{\mathrm{N}\delta}(h)
as well as
\TO_{\delta,\sigma}(\phi)
to the sum

\displaystyle\sum_{({\mathscr{M}}^{\prime},\beta)}\widetilde{h}({\mathscr{M}}^{\prime},\beta),

where
({\mathscr{M}}^{\prime},\beta)
ranges over all elements in
\Gamma\backslash X
, and
\widetilde{h}
is the function sending
({\mathscr{M}}^{\prime},\beta)
to
h(\delta^{\prime})
, where
\delta^{\prime}
corresponds to
{\mathscr{M}}^{\prime}
under
1.4
. In a line of reasoning similar to the proof of Lemma
Lemma
, we relate
\TO_{\delta,\sigma}(\phi)
to the above sum by using
1.4
, and we relate
\operatorname{O}_{\mathrm{N}\delta}(h)
to the above sum by using completed unramified descent, c.f.
[
44
, Proposition 4.7]
.
∎

4.3.

At this point, we introduce the
Lubin–Tate tower
. Let
\delta
be in
B_{n}
. By the Dieudonné–Manin classification
[
34
, (2.4.5)]
, the isomorphism class of
\breve{H}_{\delta}
is independent of our choice of
\delta
, and we may take
\delta
to lie in
\GL_{n}(F)
. We write
\breve{H}
for
\breve{H}_{\delta}
. The inverse system
(\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m})_{m}
does not depend on
\delta
either, so we shall rewrite it as

\displaystyle\dotsm\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{m}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\dotsm\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{1}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}.

This is the
Lubin–Tate tower
. Of course, all our statements from §
1
concerning
\breve{H}_{\delta}
also apply to the Lubin–Tate tower:

\bullet

this inverse system has a right action of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
,

\bullet

we can form the cohomology group
R^{i}\psi_{m}\coloneqq H^{i}(\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{m,{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})
, which is isomorphic to
H^{0}(\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{m,\overline{\kappa}},R^{i}\Psi_{\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{m,{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}}},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})
,

\bullet

we can form the direct limit
R^{i}\psi\coloneqq\varinjlim_{m}R^{i}\psi_{m}
,

\bullet

we can take the alternating sum
[R\psi]\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{i}R^{i}\psi
.

We shall now explain how the left action of
I_{F}
on
R^{i}\psi_{m}
actually extends to an action of
(B^{\times}\times W_{F})_{0}
, where
(B^{\times}\times W_{F})_{0}
is the subgroup

\displaystyle(B^{\times}\times W_{F})_{0}\coloneqq\{b\times\tau\in B^{\times}\times W_{F}|v(b)+v(\tau)=0\}

of
B^{\times}\times W_{F}
. We start by describing the action for
b\times\tau
with non-negative
v(\tau)=-v(b)
. Let
r=v(\tau)=v(b^{-1})
, and note that
b^{-1}
lies in
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
. By abuse of notation, write
b^{-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\breve{H}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{H}
for the corresponding endomorphism of
\breve{H}
, write
\breve{F}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sigma^{*}\breve{H}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\breve{H}
for the Frobenius of
\breve{H}
, and write
f_{\tau}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sigma^{-r,*}\breve{H}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\breve{H}
for the isomorphism from
1.13
. Using the isomorphism
\sigma^{*}\breve{H}\cong\breve{H}
obtained from the fact that
\delta
lies in
\GL_{n}(F)
, the map
\breve{F}
corresponds to an element of valuation
1
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
. Therefore the quasi-isogeny

\displaystyle b\times\tau\coloneqq f_{\tau}\circ(\sigma^{-r,*}\breve{F}^{-1}\circ\dotsb\circ\sigma^{-1,*}\breve{F}^{-1})\circ b^{-1}

corresponds to an element of valuation
0
and hence equals an automorphism of
\breve{H}
.

As for
b\times\tau
with negative
v(\tau)
, we define
b\times\tau
as the inverse of
d^{-1}\times\tau^{-1}
. Since we view
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
as acting from the right on
\breve{H}
, we see that in all cases
(b\times\tau)\circ(b^{\prime}\times\tau^{\prime})=bb^{\prime}\times\tau\tau^{\prime}
.

By sending the triple
(H^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
to
(H^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}\circ(d\times\tau))
, we obtain a right action on
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{m}
and hence its base change
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{m,{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}}}
. Taking cohomology gives us the desired left action on
R^{i}\psi_{m}
. Forming the direct limit over all
m
yields a
(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{\times})\times I_{F}
-admissible/continuous representation as in
[
20
, p. 24]
of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times(B^{\times}\times W_{F})_{0}
over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
.

4.4.

Now return to the situation of an arbitrary
\delta
in
B_{n}
. We shall begin studying the relationship between
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
and the Lubin–Tate tower by considering the virtual representations
[R\psi_{\delta}]
and
[R\psi]
.

Proposition
.

We have an equality of traces

\displaystyle\tr(\tau\times h|[R\psi_{\delta}])=\tr(h\times(\mathrm{N}\delta)^{-1}\times\tau|[R\psi]).

Proof.

Under our
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
-equivariant isomorphism
\breve{H}_{\delta}\cong\breve{H}
, we see that
\breve{F}
corresponds to
\delta\circ\sigma^{\oplus n}
. Therefore work from the proof of Lemma
Lemma
shows that

\displaystyle(\mathrm{N}\delta,\tau)=f_{\tau}\circ(\sigma^{-r,*}\breve{F}^{-1}\circ\dotsb\circ\sigma^{-1,*}\breve{F}^{-1})\circ\left((\breve{F}\circ\dotsb\circ\sigma^{r-1,*}\breve{F}\circ\sigma^{r,*}f)^{-1}\right)^{-1}=f_{\tau}\circ\sigma^{r,*}f,

where
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sigma^{-r,*}\breve{H}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\breve{H}
corresponds to the action of
\sigma^{-r}
. The description of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\delta,m}
in
1.13
shows that precomposing with
f_{\tau}\circ\sigma^{r,*}f
induces the action of
\tau
on
R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m}
. Thus the action of
\gamma\times(\mathrm{N}\delta)^{-1}\times\tau
on
R^{i}\psi_{m}
corresponds to the action of
\tau\times\gamma
on
R^{i}\psi_{\delta,m}
for all
\gamma
in
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
. Taking the direct limit over all
m
, forming the alternating sum over all
i
, and taking traces yields the desired result.
∎

4.5.

Before turning to the proof of the
n=1
case of Theorem A, we take a brief interlude to describe the cohomology of the Lubin–Tate tower in terms of the Lubin–Tate proof of local class field theory. Note that in the
n=1
case,
\GL_{1}({\mathcal{O}})={\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
and
(B^{\times}\times W_{F})_{0}=(F^{\times}\times W_{F})_{0}
. Recall that
\Art\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F^{\times}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}W^{\text{ab}}_{F}
denotes the local reciprocity isomorphism that sends uniformizers to geometric
q
-Frobenii. We denote the maximal unramified extension of
F
using
F^{\text{nr}}
, and we denote the extension of
F^{\text{nr}}
corresponding to
({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})^{\times}
using
F^{\text{nr},\,m}
.

In our calculations for the
n=1
case, we take
\delta=\varpi
.

Proposition
.

In the
n=1
case, we have an equality of representations

\displaystyle[R\psi]=C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}),

where
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
acts by inverse left multiplication, and
(F^{\times}\times W_{F})_{0}
acts via right multiplication by
\Art^{-1}(\tau)^{-1}b
.

We only distinguish between left and right multiplication to maintain the situation for general
n\geq 1
—there is no difference between them in the
n=1
case, because all groups involved only act through their abelianizations.

Proof of Proposition
4.5
.

Recall from §
1
that
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{m}=\Spf\breve{R}_{m}
for some regular complete local Noetherian
\breve{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
\breve{R}_{m}
. The Lubin–Tate proof of local class field theory shows that
\breve{R}_{m}
is isomorphic to the completion of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\text{nr},\,m}
, where
{\mathcal{O}}^{\text{nr},\,m}
is the ring of integers of
F^{\text{nr},\,m}
. Therefore
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{m,{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}}=\Spf\breve{R}_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{\breve{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}
consists of one point for each element of
\Gal(\breve{R}_{m}[\frac{1}{\varpi}]/\breve{F})=({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})^{\times}
.

Under this identification, the right action of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
on
\breve{\mathfrak{X}}_{m,{\mathbb{C}}_{\varpi}}
is given by sending
g
to
\gamma^{-1}g
for any
\gamma
in
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
, and the Lubin–Tate proof of local class field theory implies that the right action of
(F^{\times}\times W_{F})_{0}
is given by sending
g
to
g\Art^{-1}(\tau)b^{-1}
for any
\tau\times b
in
(F^{\times}\times W_{F})_{0}
. Taking cohomology shows that

\displaystyle R^{0}\psi_{m}=\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}[({\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m})^{\times}],

where
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
acts by inverse left multiplication, and
(F^{\times}\times W_{F})_{0}
acts via right multiplication by
\Art^{-1}(\tau)^{-1}b
. Finally, as
R^{i}\psi_{m}
vanishes for
i>n-1=0
, we have
[R\psi]=R^{0}\psi
. Forming the direct limit over all
m
yields the desired result, where we have identified
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
and
{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

4.6.

With Proposition
4.4
and Proposition
4.5
in hand, we can now prove the
n=1
case of Theorem A. This amounts to rewriting the Lubin–Tate tower in terms of the Lubin–Tate proof of local class field theory.

Proposition
.

Let
\chi
be an irreducible smooth representation of
\GL_{1}(F)
, that is, a smooth character
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F^{\times}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
. Then Theorem A holds for
\chi
, that is, there exists a unique continuous character
\rho(\chi)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W_{F}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying the following property:

\displaystyle\mbox{for all }\tau\mbox{ in }W_{F}\mbox{ with }v(\tau)>0\mbox{ and }h\mbox{ in }C^{\infty}_{c}(\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}))\mbox{, we have }\tr(f_{\tau,h}|\chi)=\tr\left(\tau|\rho(\chi)\right)\tr(h|\chi).

This
\rho(\chi)
is given by
\rho(\chi)=\chi\circ\Art^{-1}
.

Note that we need not consider Theorem A.(ii) for
n=1
, since
\GL_{1}
has no proper parabolic subgroups. As before, we always take compatible Haar measures, but we generally omit their precise description.

Proof of Proposition
4.6
.

We first use
3.4
to show that

\displaystyle\tr(f_{\tau,h}|\chi)=\tr((\phi_{\tau,h},\sigma)|\BC(\chi))=\int_{\varpi{\mathcal{O}}_{r}^{\times}}\!\dif\delta\,\tr(\tau\times h|[R\psi_{\delta}])\chi(\mathrm{N}\delta),

and then Proposition
4.4
implies that the above integral is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{\varpi{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{r}}\!\dif\delta\,\tr(h\times(\mathrm{N}\delta)^{-1}\times\tau|[R\psi])\chi(\mathrm{N}\delta).

Since
\tr(h\times(-)^{-1}\times\tau|[R\psi])
and
\tr(h\times(\mathrm{N}-)^{-1}\times\tau|[R\psi])
have matching twisted orbital integrals by Lemma
4.2
, making the change of variables
b=\mathrm{N}\delta
gives us

\displaystyle\int_{\varpi^{r}{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}}\!\dif b\,\tr(h\times b^{-1}\times\tau|[R\psi])\chi(b).

Using Proposition
4.5
and applying the Peter–Weyl theorem to
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
(which is really just Pontryagin duality, since
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
is commutative) shows that the above integral is equal to

\displaystyle\tr(h|\chi)\tr(\Art^{-1}(\tau)|\chi)=\tr(h|\chi)\tr(\tau|\chi\circ\Art^{-1}).

Therefore
\rho(\chi)=\chi\circ\Art^{-1}
satisfies the desired property. Since
\chi
is a character, it equals its trace, so it is also characterized uniquely by this property.
∎

4.7.

Now return to the arbitrary
n\geq 1
case. We introduce some notation on the multiplicity of
\lambda
in
R^{i}\psi
, where
\lambda
is an irreducible smooth representation of
B^{\times}
. Write
R^{i}\psi(\lambda)
for the vector space

\displaystyle R^{i}\psi(\lambda)\coloneqq\Hom_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{\times}}(\left.\lambda\right|_{{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{\times}},R^{i}\psi).

This has a left action of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times W_{F}
as follows. For any
f
in
R^{i}\psi(\lambda)
and
(\gamma,\tau)
in
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times W_{F}
, we set

\displaystyle((\gamma\times\tau)f)(v)=(\gamma\times b\times\tau)f(b^{-1}v)

for all
v
in
\lambda
, where
b
is any element of
B^{\times}
satisfying
v(b)+v(\tau)=0
. Since
f
commutes with
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{\times}
, this action is independent of our choice of
b
. We see that
R^{i}\psi(\lambda)
is a
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times I_{F}
-admissible/continuous representation of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times W_{F}
over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
. Write
[R\psi](\lambda)
for the virtual representation
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{i}R^{i}\psi(\lambda)
.

4.8.

In order to state our next result, we recall the
local Jacquet–Langlands correspondence
. Let
B
be a central division algebra over
F
of dimension
n^{2}
. Since every irreducible smooth representation
\lambda
of
B^{\times}
is finite-dimensional, its character distribution is represented by the function
\Theta_{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B^{\times}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{C}}
that sends
b\mapsto\tr(b|\lambda)
. This finite-dimensionality also implies that every such representation is essentially
L^{2}
.

There exists a unique bijection
[
5
, Th. 1.1]

\displaystyle\JL\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\left\{\begin{tabular}[]{c}isomorphism classes of irreducible essentially\\
$L^{2}$ representations of $\GL_{n}(F)$\end{tabular}\right\}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\left\{\begin{tabular}[]{c}isomorphism classes of irreducible\\
representations of $B^{\times}$\end{tabular}\right\}

such that, for all regular elliptic elements
\gamma
in
\GL_{n}(F)
and
b
in
B^{\times}
whose characteristic polynomials are the same, we have the equality

\displaystyle\Theta_{\pi}(\gamma)=(-1)^{n-1}\Theta_{\JL(\pi)}(b).

Furthermore, the central characters of
\pi
and
\JL(\pi)
are equal.

4.9 Proposition
.

Assume that condition (c) in Lemma
2.7
holds, and suppose that
\pi
is an irreducible cuspidal representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
. Then we have an equality of virtual
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times I_{F}
-admissible/continuous representations

\displaystyle(-1)^{n-1}\left.\pi\right|_{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\otimes\rho(\pi)=[R\psi](\JL(\pi)).

Proof.

We begin by using condition (c) in Lemma
2.7
and
3.4
to see that

\displaystyle\tr(h\times\tau|\left.\pi\right|_{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\otimes\rho(\pi))=\tr(h|\pi)\tr(\tau|\rho(\pi))=\tr(f_{\tau,h}|\pi)=\tr((\phi_{\tau,h},\sigma)|\BC(\pi)).

Then, Proposition
4.4
shows that this twisted trace is equal to the integral

\displaystyle\int_{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})}\!\dif\delta\,\tr(h\times(\mathrm{N}\delta)^{-1}\times\tau|[R\psi])\Theta_{\pi}^{\sigma}(\delta).

To narrow down our domain of integration, we shall use the following lemma.

4.10 Lemma
.

The twisted character
\Theta_{\pi}^{\sigma}
vanishes on
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\smallsetminus B_{n}
.

Proof.

Recall that we have the decomposition

\displaystyle\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})\diag(\varpi,1,\dotsc,1)\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{r})=\coprod_{k=1}^{n}B_{n,k},

and recall the description of
B_{n,k}
from
1.7
in terms of
\delta=\delta^{\circ}\oplus\delta^{\text{\'{e}t}}
. Write
P_{\delta}
for the parabolic subgroup of
\GL_{n}
over
F_{r}
associated with
\delta
. This shows that
P_{\delta}
is a proper subgroup if and only if
\delta
does not lie in
B_{n}
, and because
\pi
is cuspidal, Casselman’s theorem
[
11
, 5.2]
yields the desired result.
∎

Return to the proof of Proposition
4.9
. Lemma
4.10
implies that our integral becomes

\displaystyle\int_{B_{n}}\!\dif\delta\,\tr(h\times(\mathrm{N}\delta)^{-1}\times\tau|[R\psi])\Theta_{\pi}(\mathrm{N}\delta).

As
\tr(h\times(-)^{-1}\times\tau|[R\psi])
and
\tr(h\times(\mathrm{N}-)^{-1}\times\tau|[R\psi])
have matching twisted orbital integrals by Lemma
4.2
, making the change of variables
b=\mathrm{N}\delta
and applying the local Jacquet–Langlands correspondence indicate that the above expression equals

\displaystyle(-1)^{n-1}\int_{B_{r}}\!\dif b\,\tr(h\times b^{-1}\times\tau|[R\psi])\Theta_{\JL(\pi)}(b)=(-1)^{n-1}\tr(h\times\tau|[R\psi](\JL(\pi))).

Since virtual
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times I_{F}
-admissible/continuous representations of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times W_{F}
are determined by their traces, this yields the desired result.
∎

4.11.

We conclude this section by verifying that, under the assumption that condition (c) from Lemma
2.7
holds, condition (d) holds as well.

Proposition
.

Assume that condition (c) in Lemma
2.7
holds, and suppose that
\pi
is an irreducible cuspidal representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
. Then the
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\geq 0}
-virtual continuous representation
\rho(\pi)
is actually a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-virtual continuous representation of
W_{F}
.

Proof.

Proposition
4.9
shows that the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-virtual representation
[R\psi](\JL(\pi))
equals
\left.\pi\right|_{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}\otimes\rho(\pi)
as virtual
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times I_{F}
-admissible/continuous representations of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\times W_{F}
over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
. Thus its
\lambda
-isotypic component is also a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-virtual representation for any irreducible smooth representation
\lambda
of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
. If we could find such a
\lambda
that is contained in
\pi
with multiplicity
1
, then the
\lambda
-isotypic component of
[R\psi](\JL(\pi))
would equal
\rho(\pi)
. Therefore, once we prove the following lemma, the result follows.
∎

4.12 Lemma
.

Let
\pi
be an irreducible cuspidal representation of
\GL_{n}(F)
. Then there exists an irreducible smooth representation
\lambda
of
\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
such that
\pi
contains
\lambda
with multiplicity
1
.

Proof.

Since
\pi
is cuspidal and hence generic, the theory of new-vectors
[
29
, Theorem (5.1).(ii)]
shows that there exists a non-negative number
a
such that
\dim\pi^{K(a)}=1
, where
K(a)
is the subgroup

\displaystyle K(a)\coloneqq\left\{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right]\in\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})\middle|a\in\GL_{n-1}({\mathcal{O}}),\,c\equiv 0\pmod{\varpi^{a}},\,d\equiv 1\pmod{\varpi^{a}}\right\}

for positive
a
, and
K(a)\coloneqq\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})
for
a=0
. In other words,
\dim\Hom_{K(a)}({\mathbb{C}},\left.\pi\right|_{K(a)})=1
, where
{\mathbb{C}}
denotes the trivial representation. Frobenius reciprocity yields

\displaystyle\dim\Hom_{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}(\cInd_{K(a)}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}{\mathbb{C}},\left.\pi\right|_{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})})=\dim\Hom_{K(a)}({\mathbb{C}},\left.\pi\right|_{K(a)})=1.

The Peter–Weyl theorem then implies that any irreducible smooth subrepresentation
\lambda
of
\cInd_{K(a)}^{\GL_{n}({\mathcal{O}})}{\mathbb{C}}
is contained in
\pi
with multiplicity
1
, as desired.
∎

5.
Moduli spaces of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves

At this point, we shift our focus from local considerations to global ones. In this section, we begin by introducing
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves
, which are the equi-characteristic analog of abelian varieties equipped with certain endomorphism structures. Afterwards, we introduce moduli spaces of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves, which therefore correspond to certain Shimura varieties and their integral models. The cohomology of these moduli spaces plays an important role in the Langlands correspondence. We can also obtain local shtukas from
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves, and this is the equi-characteristic version of taking the
p
-divisible group of an abelian variety. We conclude this section by introducing a
Serre–Tate theorem
, which relates deformations of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves to deformations of their associated local shtukas. This will eventually allow us to prove condition (c) in Lemma
2.7
.

5.1.

We start by switching our notation to a global context. In these next few sections, let
\kappa
be a finite field of characteristic
p
and cardinality
q
, and fix a separable closure
\overline{\kappa}
of
\kappa
. We view all separable extensions of
\kappa
as lying in
\overline{\kappa}
. Let
C
be a geometrically connected proper smooth curve over
\kappa
, and write
\mathbf{F}
for its field of rational functions. Denote the adele ring of
C
using
{\mathbb{A}}
, denote the ring of integers of
{\mathbb{A}}
using
{\mathbb{O}}
, and for any finite closed subscheme
I
of
C
, write
{\mathbb{K}}_{I}
for the ideal of
{\mathbb{O}}
corresponding to
I
.

For any place
x
of
C
, write
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
for the local ring given by the completion of
C
at
x
, and write
\mathbf{F}_{x}
for the fraction field of
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
. Then
\mathbf{F}_{x}
is a completion of
\mathbf{F}
at
x
. Choose a uniformizer
\varpi_{x}
of
x
in
\mathbf{F}
, write
\kappa_{x}
for
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}/\varpi_{x}
, write
q_{x}
for
\#\kappa_{x}
, and write
\deg{x}
for
[\kappa_{x}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\kappa]
. By abuse of notation, we write
x
for the normalized valuation corresponding to
x
. We also write
\deg\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{Z}}
for the function given by sending

\displaystyle(a_{x})_{x}\mapsto-\sum_{x}x(a_{x})\deg{x}.

We fix a closed point
\infty
in
C
, which shall serve as a replacement for the archimedean places at infinity.

5.2.

Since division algebras are fundamental to our constructions, we recall some facts about them here. For any central division algebra
D
over
\mathbf{F}
, write
D_{x}
for
D\otimes_{\mathbf{F}}\mathbf{F}_{x}
, and write
\inv_{x}(D)
for the Hasse invariant of
D_{x}
. Recall that the Brauer group of
\mathbf{F}
fits into an exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\Br(\mathbf{F})\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\bigoplus_{x}\Br(\mathbf{F}_{x})=\bigoplus_{x}{\mathbb{Q}}/{\mathbb{Z}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{Q}}/{\mathbb{Z}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}0,

where
x
runs over all places of
C
, the first map sends
D\mapsto(D_{x})_{x}
, and the second map sums the Hasse invariants of any element in
\bigoplus_{x}\Br(\mathbf{F}_{x})
. In particular, we have
\inv_{x}(D)=0
for cofinitely many
x
, that is,
D
is split at
x
for cofinitely many
x
. For any central division algebra
D
in
\Br(\mathbf{F})
, its dimension equals the square of the least common denominator of
\inv_{x}(D)
as
x
runs over all places of
\mathbf{F}
.

We now fix a central division algebra
D
over
\mathbf{F}
of dimension
n^{2}
such that
\inv_{\infty}(D)=0
, and we write
Bad
for the set of places
x
of
C
where
\inv_{x}(D)\neq 0
, that is, where
D
ramifies. Then
Bad
is finite, and
\infty
does not lie in
Bad
. We write
C^{\prime}
for the open subscheme
C\smallsetminus\text{Bad}
of
C
.

5.3.

Next, we introduce
orders
of
D
, which shall provide some sort of integrality structure on
D
.

Definition
.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}
be a locally free (not necessarily commutative)
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}
-algebra of rank
n^{2}
. We say
{\mathscr{D}}
is an
order
of
D
if its generic fiber
{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathbf{F}}
is isomorphic to
D
. For an order
{\mathscr{D}}
of
D
, we say
{\mathscr{D}}
is
maximal
if it is maximal with respect to injective
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}
-algebra morphisms
{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}{\mathscr{D}}
between orders of
D
.

We denote the completion of
{\mathscr{D}}
at
x
using
{\mathscr{D}}_{x}
, which is a free
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
-algebra of rank
n^{2}
, and we identify its generic fiber
{\mathscr{D}}_{x}[\frac{1}{\varpi_{x}}]
with
D_{x}
.

5.4.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}
be an order of
D
. Let
U
be an affine open subset of
C
, and let
M
be an
\mathbf{F}
-basis of
D
contained in
{\mathscr{D}}(U)
. Because
D
splits at cofinitely many places, checking valuations shows that
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}\cdot M={\mathscr{D}}_{x}
for cofinitely many
x
. Conversely, let
({\mathscr{D}}_{x})_{x}
be a collection of
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
-orders (where
x
ranges over all places of
C
) such that there exists an
\mathbf{F}
-basis
M
of
D
for which
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}\cdot M={\mathscr{D}}_{x}
for cofinitely many
x
. One can use the Riemann–Roch theorem to show that the Zariski sheaf

\displaystyle U\mapsto\bigcap_{x^{\prime}}{\mathscr{D}}_{x^{\prime}}\cap D,

where
x^{\prime}
runs over all closed points in
U
, is an order of
D
.

This construction yields a bijection between isomorphism classes of orders of
D
and collections
({\mathscr{M}}_{x})_{x}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
-orders such that there exists an
\mathbf{F}
-basis
M
of
D
satisfying
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}\cdot M={\mathscr{M}}_{x}
for cofinitely many
x
. Note that
{\mathscr{D}}
is maximal if and only if every
{\mathscr{M}}_{x}
is maximal.

5.5.

From now on, fix a maximal order
{\mathscr{D}}
of
D
. We may now introduce
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves. For any scheme
S
over
\kappa
, write
\sigma
for the absolute
q
-Frobenius on
S
, and write
σ
for
(\id_{C}\times_{\kappa}\sigma)^{*}
.

Definition
.

Let
S
be a scheme over
\kappa
. A
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaf
over
S
is a commutative diagram of sheaves on
C\times_{\kappa}S

\textstyle{\dotsm\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{i-2}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{E}}_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{i-1}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{E}}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{i}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{E}}_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{i+1}}
\textstyle{\dotsm}
\textstyle{\dotsm\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!j_{i-2}}
\scriptstyle{t_{i-2}}
\textstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!{\mathscr{E}}_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!j_{i-1}}
\scriptstyle{t_{i-1}}
\textstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!{\mathscr{E}}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!j_{i}}
\scriptstyle{t_{i}}
\textstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!{\mathscr{E}}_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!j_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{t_{i+1}}
\textstyle{\dotsm}

where the
{\mathscr{E}}_{i}
are locally free right
{\mathscr{D}}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}
-modules of rank
1
(and hence vector bundles over
S
of rank
n^{2}
), and the
t_{i}
and
j_{i}
are injective morphisms of right
{\mathscr{D}}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}
-modules satisfying the following conditions:

\bullet

for all
i
,
{\mathscr{E}}_{i+n\deg\infty}
is isomorphic to
{\mathscr{E}}_{i}(\infty)
, and this isomorphism identifies
{\mathscr{E}}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}{\mathscr{E}}_{i+n\deg\infty}
with the canonical injection
{\mathscr{E}}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}{\mathscr{E}}_{i}(\infty)
,

\bullet

there exists a morphism
i_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\infty
such that
\coker j_{1}
is supported on the image of its graph
\Gamma_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\infty\times_{\kappa}S
,

\bullet

there exists a morphism
i_{o}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}C^{\prime}\smallsetminus\infty
such that
\coker t_{1}
is supported on the image of its graph
\Gamma_{o}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}C\times_{\kappa}S
,

\bullet

when viewed as
{\mathscr{O}}_{S}
-modules, the
\coker{j_{i}}
and
\coker{t_{i}}
are locally free of rank
n
.

We denote
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves by
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
. We say that
i_{\infty}
is the
pole
of
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
, and we say that
i_{o}
is the
zero
of
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
.

Since the image of
i_{o}
is disjoint from
\infty
, we see
t_{i}
induces an isomorphism
{}^{\sigma}\!({\mathscr{E}}_{i}/{\mathscr{E}}_{i-1})\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}{\mathscr{E}}_{i+1}/{\mathscr{E}}_{i}
. Thus
\coker j_{i+1}
is supported on the image of the graph of
i_{\infty}\circ\sigma^{i}
. And as the image of
i_{o}
is disjoint from
\infty
, we see that
\coker t_{i}
is supported on the image of
\Gamma_{o}
for all
i
.

5.6.

We proceed to describe the
moduli space
of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves. For any scheme
S
over
\kappa
, write
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}(S)
for the category whose objects are
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves and whose morphisms are isomorphisms of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves. Then
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}
forms an fppf stack over
\kappa

[
35
, (2.4)]
, and the assignment sending a
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaf over
S
to its zero
i_{o}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}C^{\prime}\smallsetminus\infty
yields a morphism
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}C^{\prime}\smallsetminus\infty
. We have the following result of Laumon–Rapoport–Stuhler.

Proposition

(
[
35
, (4.1)]
)
.

Our
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}
is Deligne–Mumford stack, and the morphism
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}C^{\prime}\smallsetminus\infty
is smooth of relative dimension
n-1
.

This morphism
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}C^{\prime}\smallsetminus\infty
is the equi-characteristic analog of the structure morphism from an integral model of a Shimura variety to
\Spec{\mathbb{Z}}
. Given that
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
correspond to abelian schemes
A\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}S
in our analogy, the zero of
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
therefore corresponds to the characteristic of
S
.

5.7.

Just as with abelian varieties, we have a notion of
level structure
for
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves. The situation is more complicated when the characteristic divides the level, so we begin by excluding this case. Let
I
be a finite closed subscheme of
C\smallsetminus\infty
.

Definition
.

Let
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
be
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaf over
S
such that the image of
i_{o}
does not meet
I
. A
level-
I
structure
on
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
is an isomorphism of right
\left.{\mathscr{D}}\right|_{I}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}
-modules

\displaystyle\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\left.{\mathscr{D}}\right|_{I}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\left.{\mathscr{E}}\right|_{I\times_{\kappa}S}

such that the diagram

\textstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!(\left.{\mathscr{D}}\right|_{I}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!\iota}
\textstyle{\left.{\mathscr{D}}\right|_{I}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!\left.{\mathscr{E}}\right|_{I\times_{\kappa}S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left.j\right|_{I\times_{\kappa}S}}
\textstyle{\left.{\mathscr{E}}\right|_{I\times_{\kappa}S}}

commutes, where
f_{\sigma}
denotes the linearization of
\id\times_{\kappa}\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\left.{\mathscr{D}}\right|_{I}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\left.{\mathscr{D}}\right|_{I}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}
. For any closed subscheme
I^{\prime}
of
I
and level-
I
structure
\iota
, its restriction
\left.\iota\right|_{I^{\prime}}
is a level-
I^{\prime}
structure.

5.8.

We now incorporate the data of level-
I
structures in order to introduce more moduli spaces of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves. Write
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}(S)
for the category whose

\bullet

objects are pairs
(({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i},\iota)
, where
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
is a
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaf over
S
, and
\iota
is a level-
I
structure on
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
,

\bullet

morphisms are isomorphisms of pairs.

Then
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}
forms a Deligne–Mumford stack over
\kappa

[
35
, (4.1)]
. We naturally identify
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},\varnothing}={\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}
, and as in
5.6
, we have a morphism
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}C^{\prime}\smallsetminus(I\cup\infty)
given by sending any
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaf with level-
I
structure to its zero.

By abuse of notation, we shall write
{\mathscr{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{O}}/{\mathbb{K}}_{I}
for the product ring
\prod_{x}{\mathscr{D}}_{x}/{\mathbb{K}}_{I,x}{\mathscr{D}}_{x}
, where
x
runs over all places in
C
. Since
{\mathbb{K}}_{I,x}={\mathcal{O}}_{x}
for cofinitely many
x
, we see that
{\mathscr{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{O}}/{\mathbb{K}}_{I}
is finite. As
I
varies, the stacks
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}
are related as follows.

Proposition

(
[
35
, (4.1)]
)
.

For any closed subscheme
I^{\prime}
of
I
, the restriction morphism
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\left.{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I^{\prime}}\right|_{C^{\prime}\smallsetminus(I\cup\infty)}
is finite representable Galois, and the right action of

\displaystyle\ker\left(({\mathscr{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{O}}/{\mathbb{K}}_{I})^{\times}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}({\mathscr{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{O}}/{\mathbb{K}}_{I^{\prime}})^{\times}\right)

on
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\left.{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I^{\prime}}\right|_{C^{\prime}\smallsetminus(I\cup\infty)}
given by multiplication on the level-
I
structure yields the Galois action.

By combining this with Proposition
5.6
, we see that
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}C^{\prime}\smallsetminus(I\cup\infty)
is smooth of relative dimension
n-1
.

5.9.

In anticipation for studying the cohomology of
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}
, we present some operations that one can perform on
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}
. First, write
\Pic_{I}(C)
for the group

\displaystyle\Pic_{I}(C)\coloneqq\{\mbox{pairs }({\mathscr{L}},\beta)\}/\!\sim,

where
{\mathscr{L}}
is a line bundle on
C
, and
\beta
is an isomorphism
\beta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathscr{O}}_{I}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}\left.{\mathscr{L}}\right|_{I}
of
{\mathscr{O}}_{I}
-modules, under tensor product. As
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves consist of vector bundles, we can twist them by line bundles. That is, for any
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaf
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
over
S
with level-
I
structure
\iota
, we obtain another
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaf over
S
with level-
I
structure as follows:

\displaystyle({\mathscr{L}},\beta)\cdot(({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i},\iota)\coloneqq(({\mathscr{E}}_{i}\otimes({\mathscr{L}}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}),t_{i}\otimes\id,j_{i}\otimes\id)_{i},\iota\otimes\beta).

This yields an action of
\Pic_{I}(C)
on
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}
over
C^{\prime}\smallsetminus(I\cup\infty)
.

5.10.

As
I
varies, the moduli spaces of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves with level-
I
structure form a system of stacks
({\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I})_{I}
, which corresponds to how Shimura varieties form systems of schemes. We package this system into one object as follows. First, let
T
be a finite set of places of
\mathbf{F}
. Write
{\mathbb{A}}^{T}
for the ring of adeles away from
T
, and more generally for any subset
X
of
{\mathbb{A}}
, write
X^{T}
for the projection of
X
to
{\mathbb{A}}^{T}
. Similarly, write
{\mathbb{A}}_{T}
for the ring of adeles at
T
, and write
X_{T}
for the projection of
X
to
{\mathbb{A}}_{T}
. We write
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}^{\infty}
for the inverse limit

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}^{\infty}\coloneqq\varprojlim_{I}{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I},

where
I
ranges over all finite closed subschemes of
C
that do not meet
\infty
. This inverse limit yields a morphism
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}^{\infty}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\Spec{\mathbf{F}}
. By abuse of notation, write
{\mathbb{O}}^{\infty}
for the
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}
-algebra given by

\displaystyle U\mapsto\prod_{x^{\prime}}{\mathcal{O}}_{x^{\prime}},

where
x^{\prime}
runs over all closed points in
U\smallsetminus\infty
. We see that
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}^{\infty}(S)
is the category whose
[
35
, (7.1)]

\bullet

objects are pairs
(({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i},\iota^{\infty})
, where
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
is a
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaf over
S
and
\iota^{\infty}
is a
({\mathscr{D}}\otimes_{{\mathscr{O}}_{C}}{\mathbb{O}}^{\infty})\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}
-linear isomorphism

\displaystyle\iota^{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}({\mathscr{D}}\otimes_{{\mathscr{O}}_{C}}{\mathbb{O}}^{\infty})\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\sim}{\mathscr{E}}\otimes({\mathbb{O}}^{\infty}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S})

such that the diagram

\textstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!(({\mathscr{D}}\otimes_{{\mathscr{O}}_{C}}{\mathbb{O}}^{\infty})\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!\iota^{\infty}}
\textstyle{({\mathscr{D}}\otimes_{{\mathscr{O}}_{C}}{\mathbb{O}}^{\infty})\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\infty}}
\textstyle{{}^{\sigma}\!{\mathscr{E}}\otimes({\mathbb{O}}^{\infty}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j\boxtimes\id}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{E}}\otimes({\mathbb{O}}^{\infty}\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S})}

commutes, where
f_{\sigma}
denotes the linearization of
\id\times_{\kappa}\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}({\mathscr{D}}\otimes_{{\mathscr{O}}_{C}}{\mathbb{O}}^{\infty})\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}({\mathscr{D}}\otimes_{{\mathscr{O}}_{C}}{\mathbb{O}}^{\infty})\boxtimes{\mathscr{O}}_{S}
,

\bullet

morphisms are isomorphisms of pairs.

5.11.

The action of twisting by line bundles is compatible with the inverse limit, so
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}^{\infty}
has an action of

\displaystyle\varprojlim_{I}\Pic_{I}(C)=\mathbf{F}^{\times}\backslash{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}/\mathbf{F}_{\infty}^{\times}=\mathbf{F}^{\infty,\times}\backslash{\mathbb{A}}^{\infty,\times}

over
\mathbf{F}
, where we identify the idele
(a_{x})_{x}
with the line bundle
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}(-\sum_{x}x(a_{x}))
along with the trivialization induced via multiplication by
(a_{x})_{x}
. In particular,
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}^{\infty}
has an action of
{\mathbb{A}}^{\infty,\times}
. We see that
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}^{\infty}
also has a right action of

\displaystyle\varprojlim_{I}({\mathscr{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{O}}/{\mathbb{K}}_{I})^{\times}=({\mathscr{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{O}}^{\infty})^{\times}

over
\mathbf{F}
given by multiplication on the level structure, and both of these actions actually arise from a right action of
(D\otimes{\mathbb{A}}^{\infty})^{\times}
on
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}^{\infty}

[
35
, (7.3)]
. This is the
Hecke
action, analogous to the Hecke action on Shimura varieties. Writing
{\mathscr{K}}_{I}
for the subgroup

\displaystyle{\mathscr{K}}_{I}\coloneqq\ker\left(({\mathscr{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{O}})^{\times}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}({\mathscr{D}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}/{\mathbb{K}}_{I})^{\times}\right),

we see that Proposition
5.8
implies
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}^{\infty}/{\mathscr{K}}_{I}^{\infty}={\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}
.

5.12.

Since
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves are essentially vector bundles, they have a notion of
degree
.

Definition
.

Let
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
be a
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaf. We say its
degree
is the locally constant function

\displaystyle\frac{\deg{\mathscr{E}}_{1}-\deg{\mathscr{D}}}{n}

on the base scheme
S
, where we use
1
instead of
0
to avoid confusion with
o
. One can show that the degree is integer-valued
[
32
, p. 49]
.

By partitioning the base scheme
S
by the degree of
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
, we can write
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}
as a disjoint union

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}=\coprod_{d}{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I,d},

where
d
ranges over all integers, and
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I,d}
is the open substack of
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaves with level-
I
structure whose degree equals
d
. Note that the restriction morphisms
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I^{\prime}}
preserve degree, so we obtain a similar decomposition

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{\mathscr{D}}^{\infty}=\coprod_{d}{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},d}^{\infty},

where
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},d}^{\infty}=\varprojlim_{I}{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I,d}
for
I
running over all finite closed subschemes of
C\smallsetminus\infty
.

5.13.

We shall define an action of
{\mathbb{Z}}
on
{\mathcal{E}}\ell\ell_{{\mathscr{D}},I}
over
C^{\prime}\smallsetminus(I\cup\infty)
as follows. For any integer
l
and
{\mathscr{D}}
-elliptic sheaf
({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
, write

\displaystyle[l]({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}\coloneqq({\mathscr{E}}_{i+l},t_{i+l},j_{i+l})_{i},

that is,
[l]
acts via translation by
l
on the index
i
. Then
[l]({\mathscr{E}}_{i},t_{i},j_{i})_{i}
also forms a
